, we see that for any
\upsilon\in M
, there exists
\upsilon_{\mathbf{f}}\in M^{\text{out}}
such that for all states
\rho
of the input logical type of
C
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\left({\mathsf{rdec}}_{C}^{\text{out}}\circ\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{f}}\circ{\mathsf{renc}}_{C}^{\text{in}}\right)\otimes\mathsf{map}[C_{\Omega}]\right)(\rho\otimes\upsilon)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{G}\otimes\mathsf{map}[C_{\Omega}]\right)(\rho)\otimes\upsilon_{\mathbf{f}}.

(4.36)

Define
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{G},\mathbf{f}}:\mathsf{L}\left({\mathsf{t}_{\mathsf{syn}}^{\text{in}}}\right)\rightarrow\mathsf{L}\left({\mathsf{t}_{\mathsf{syn}}^{\text{out}}}\right)
by
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{G},\mathbf{f}}(\upsilon)=\upsilon_{\mathbf{f}}
.
Then
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{G},\mathbf{f}}
is a valid linear map defined on
\mathsf{span}(M^{\text{in}})
.
Extend
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{G},\mathbf{f}}
linearly and arbitrarily onto the rest of the domain
\mathsf{L}\left({\mathsf{t}_{\mathsf{syn}}^{\text{in}}}\right)
.
We have that for all good input states
\sigma_{1}
,
Eq.

4.34
holds as desired.
Applying the same arguments to
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{f}}
, we can construct
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{R},\mathbf{f}}
for
Eq.

4.30
.
∎

Later on, in the proof of level reduction, the syndrome subsystem will be moved to be part of the environment circuit.

Figure 4:
Pictoral representation of the decoupling lemma (
Section

4.2
).
Whenever
C
accepts or outputs multiple wire bundles,
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{G},\mathbf{f}}
acts on all syndrome subsystems and
\mathcal{G}
acts on all logical subsystems.

4.3
Level reduction

We are now ready to prove an analog of “level reduction” from
[
AGP05
]
which represents the most challenging form of composition.
Roughly, this allows reasoning about the simulated circuit whenever gadgets in the fault-tolerant circuit fail.
The proof will crucially use the friendliness of the gadgets and the environment circuit.

{theorem}

[Level reduction]
Fix a circuit
C=(V,E,\mathsf{conn},\mathsf{type},\mathsf{gate})
(potentially with environment) and a contraction map
\kappa\colon V\to V_{\kappa}
such that every location in
W=V_{\kappa}
of the contracted circuit
C_{\kappa}=(W,E_{\kappa},\mathsf{conn}_{\kappa},\mathsf{type}_{\kappa},\mathsf{gate}_{\kappa})
corresponds to a gadget
w\in W
.
Assign a bundling
\beta\colon E\to E_{\beta}
to the edges of
C_{\kappa}
compatible with the bundling of each gadget and a code specification
{\mathsf{spec}}
such that each gadget
w\in W
simulates the gate
\mathcal{G}_{w}
with input and output code types
23
23

23

Actually, they need only be compatible with the code type in the sense that the code type assigned to the wire bundle may be a stronger set of bad error supports.
given by the global spec
{\mathsf{spec}}
and bad Pauli faults
\mathcal{F}_{w}\subseteq P(\kappa^{-1}(w))
.
We further require that: 1) The input and output types of the circuit (with respect to
{\mathsf{spec}}
) are trivial (see
Section

4
).
2) All gadgets
w\in W
connected to the output have no bad fault paths
\mathcal{F}_{w}=\{\}
e.g. are classical decoders (classical locations do not have faults).
24
24

24

This is possible for circuits with classical input and output in the model of computation where classical circuits do not have faults. More generally, the corresponding statement requires encoders on the input and decoders on the output.

Consider also the logical circuit
C_{L}=(W,E_{L},\mathsf{conn}_{L},\mathsf{type}_{L},\mathsf{gate}_{L})
where edge bundle
e_{\beta}\in E_{\beta}
of
C_{\kappa}
is replaced by an edge of the corresponding logical type (given by
{\mathsf{spec}}
) and, for every gadget
w\in W
of
C_{\kappa}
, the gate
\mathsf{gate}_{\kappa}(w)
is replaced by the gate that the gadget simulates
\mathsf{gate}_{L}(w):=\mathcal{G}_{w}
.

Now fix a Pauli fault
\mathbf{f}
supported on
C
and a family of bad fault paths
\mathcal{W}\subseteq P(W)
.
If
\mathbf{f}
is
\mathcal{W}\bullet\{\mathcal{F}_{w}\}_{w\in W}
-avoiding, then, for some
\mathcal{W}
-avoiding fault
25
25

25

\mathbf{f}^{\prime}
is not Pauli in general.

\mathbf{f}^{\prime}
supported on
C_{L,\Omega}
(
C_{L}

with environment
) we have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{map}[C[\mathbf{f}]]=\mathsf{map}[C_{L,\Omega}[\mathbf{f}^{\prime}]]

(4.37)

Proof.

Let
U\subseteq W
be the set of “good” gadgets where, for each gadget
w\in U
, the fault
\mathbf{f}
is
\mathcal{F}_{w}
-avoiding on the locations of the gadget
\kappa^{-1}(w)
.
We will construct
C_{L,\Omega}[\mathbf{f}^{\prime}]
by a sequence of circuit rewrites of
C_{\kappa}[\mathbf{f}]
.

The first step will be to rewrite the gates of
C_{\kappa}[\mathbf{f}]
into a new gate map
\mathsf{gate}^{(1)}_{\kappa}
.
First note that, because
\mathbf{f}
is Pauli, we can take the vertex contraction to be trivial.
Thus, in what follows,
C
may have an environment circuit, but we do not need to explicitly work with it.
Let
\widetilde{\mathsf{gate}}_{\kappa}(w)
be the gate map of
C_{\kappa}[\mathbf{f}]
.

For each gadget
w\in W
, if the gadget is good
w\in U
, then the support of the fault
\mathbf{f}
is
\mathcal{F}_{w}
-avoiding and can we apply the decomposition (
Eq.

4.29
and
Eq.

4.18
) of the decoupling lemma
Section

4.2
.
For input edge bundles
(b_{1},\dots,b_{\ell_{w}})
of
w
, the faulty gadget map has the decomposition

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{gate}}_{\kappa}(w)={\mathsf{renc}}^{\text{out}}_{w}\circ\left[\mathcal{G}_{w}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{G},\mathbf{f},w}\right]\circ{\mathsf{rdec}}^{\text{in}}_{w}\circ\left(\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\text{in}}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathcal{R}_{\ell_{w}}^{\text{in}}\right).

(4.38)

We will “push” the superoperators
\mathcal{R}_{i}
to the preceding gadget.
More precisely, consider a gadget
w\in W
with
m_{w}
output bundles
(b_{1},\dots b_{m_{w}})
.
For
i\in[{m_{w}}]
, let
u_{i}\in W
be the gadget associated with
\mathsf{dest}(b_{i})
.
If
u_{i}
is good
u_{i}\in U
, then define
\mathcal{T}_{w,i}^{\text{out}}
to be the corresponding input bundle filter in the decomposition
Eq.

4.38
at
u_{i}
.
Otherwise, we take
\mathcal{T}_{w,i}^{\text{out}}
to be the identity.
We can now write the modified gate map
\mathsf{gate}_{\kappa}^{(1)}
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{gate}_{\kappa}^{(1)}(w)=\begin{cases}\left(\mathcal{T}_{w,1}^{\text{out}}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathcal{T}_{w,{m_{w}}}^{\text{out}}\right)\circ\widetilde{\mathsf{gate}}_{\kappa}(w)&w\notin U\\
\left(\mathcal{T}_{w,1}^{\text{out}}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathcal{T}_{w,{m_{w}}}^{\text{out}}\right)\circ{\mathsf{renc}}^{\text{out}}_{w}\circ\left[\mathcal{G}_{w}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{G},\mathbf{f},w}\right]\circ{\mathsf{rdec}}^{\text{in}}_{w}&w\in U\end{cases}

(4.39)

By construction, the contracted circuit
C_{\kappa}^{(1)}=(W,E_{\kappa},\mathsf{conn}_{\kappa},\mathsf{type}_{\kappa},\mathsf{gate}_{\kappa}^{(1)})
with the modified gate map is equivalent to
C_{\kappa}[\mathbf{f}]
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{map}[C_{\kappa}^{(1)}]=\mathsf{map}[C_{\kappa}[\mathbf{f}]].

(4.40)

In the next step, we will strip off the filter superoperators where we are able to.
For each gadget
w\in W
, let
\mathcal{B}^{\text{in}}_{w}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\text{out}}_{w}
be the input and output bad error supports of the gadget
w
(induced by
{\mathsf{spec}}
).
Consider the action of
\mathsf{map}[C_{\kappa}]
on an arbitrary input state
\rho_{0}
.
Let
\rho_{t}
,
t\in[|W|]
be the state after applying the gate maps of gadgets in
(1,\dots,t)
(in topological order) to
\rho_{0}
.

For each
t\in[|W|]
, the direct ancestors
u
of
w_{t}
have the filter superoperators
\left(\mathcal{T}_{u,1}^{\text{out}}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathcal{T}_{u,{m_{u}}}^{\text{out}}\right)
, so there is a logical state
\sigma_{t-1}
such that
\rho_{t-1}
is
\mathcal{B}^{\text{in}}_{w_{t}}
-deviated from
\mathsf{enc}_{w_{t}}^{\text{in}}(\sigma_{t-1})
26
26

26

Here the input encoder of
w_{t}
is defined by
{\mathsf{spec}}
extended by identity for the subsystems not in the support of
\mathsf{gate}_{\kappa}(w_{t})
.
Thus, if
w_{t}\in U
is good, using the simulation definition (
Section

4.1
), it follows that
\rho_{t}
is
\mathcal{B}^{\text{out}}_{w_{t}}
-deviated from
\mathsf{enc}_{w_{t}}^{\text{out}}\circ\left(\mathcal{G}_{w_{t}}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{G},\mathbf{f},w_{t}}\right)(\sigma_{t-1})
.
From
Eq.

4.30
of
Section

4.2
, we see that on such a good logical state, the filter can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\forall i\in[m_{w_{t}}],\mathcal{T}_{w_{t},i}^{\text{out}}

\displaystyle={\mathsf{renc}}^{\text{out}}_{w_{t}}\circ\left[I\otimes\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{T},\mathbf{f},w_{t},i}\right]\circ{\mathsf{rdec}}^{\text{out}}_{w_{t}}.

(4.41)

In other words, the filter superoperators at
w_{t}
(if present) act as identity on the logical state.
We can therefore absorb the filter superoperators into the fault superoperator supported on the syndrome system at each good gadget.
Denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{T},\mathbf{f},w_{t}}

\displaystyle:=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{m_{w_{t}}}\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{T},\mathbf{f},w_{t},i}\qquad\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{f},w_{t}}:=\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{T},\mathbf{f},w_{t}}\circ\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{G},\mathbf{f},w_{t}}

(4.42)

we can define another modified gate map

\displaystyle\mathsf{gate}_{\kappa}^{(2)}(w)=\begin{cases}\left(\mathcal{T}_{w,1}^{\text{out}}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathcal{T}_{w,{m_{w}}}^{\text{out}}\right)\circ\widetilde{\mathsf{gate}}_{\kappa}(w)&w\notin U\\
{\mathsf{renc}}^{\text{out}}_{w}\circ\left[\mathcal{G}_{w}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{f},w}\right]\circ{\mathsf{rdec}}^{\text{in}}_{w}&w\in U\end{cases}

(4.43)

By induction on the circuit locations in reverse topological order, and noting that the input code type is trivial so the base case is satisfied, we see that this rewrite of gate map results in an equivalent circuit
C_{\kappa}^{(2)}=(W,E_{\kappa},\mathsf{conn}_{\kappa},\mathsf{type}_{\kappa},\mathsf{gate}_{\kappa}^{(2)})
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{map}[C_{\kappa}^{(2)}]=\mathsf{map}[C_{\kappa}^{(1)}]

(4.44)

We will now unencode every edge bundle of
C_{\kappa}^{(2)}
by inserting a reversible encoder/decoder pair for each edge bundle
b
.
This replaces the edges of the edge bundle by two edges, one with the logical type of
b
and one with the syndrome type of
b
.
The reversible encoders and decoders cancel the reversible decoders and encoders of the good gadgets.

\displaystyle\mathsf{gate}_{\kappa}^{(3)}(w)=\begin{cases}{\mathsf{rdec}}^{\text{out}}_{w}\circ\left(\mathcal{T}_{w,1}^{\text{out}}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathcal{T}_{w,{m_{w}}}^{\text{out}}\right)\circ\widetilde{\mathsf{gate}}_{\kappa}(w)\circ{\mathsf{renc}}^{\text{in}}_{w}&w\notin U\\
\mathcal{G}_{w}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{f},w}&w\in U\end{cases}

(4.45)

We have only inserted pairs of unitaries and their inverses.

\displaystyle\mathsf{map}[C_{\kappa}^{(3)}]=\mathsf{map}[C_{\kappa}^{(2)}]

(4.46)

Finally, we can construct
C_{L,\mathcal{E}}[\mathbf{f}^{\prime}]
by “splitting” all good gadgets of
C_{\kappa}^{(3)}
:
Every good gadget
w
has a gate
\mathsf{gate}_{\kappa}^{(3)}(w)=\mathcal{G}_{w}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{f},w}
that factorizes into a superoperator supported only on the logical subsystem
\mathcal{G}_{w}
and a superoperator supported only on the syndrome subsystem
\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{f},w}
.

We now equip
C_{L}
with an environment circuit
C_{\Omega}
with network matching that of
C_{L}
.
That is, for every vertex
w
of
C_{L}
, we add a vertex
w^{(\mathrm{s})}
to the environment circuit.
The edge types are the syndrome types of each bundle of
C_{\kappa}
(which are in bijection with edges of
C_{L}
) and the gate map is arbitrary.
The fault
\mathbf{f}^{\prime}
contracts
w^{(\mathrm{s})}
with
w
whenever
w\notin U
is not good.
More precisely, let
\widetilde{\mathsf{gate}}_{L}
be the gate map of
C_{L,\mathcal{E}}[\mathbf{f}^{\prime}]
.

\displaystyle w\in U

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{gate}}_{L}(w)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{w}

(4.47)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{gate}}_{L}(w^{\mathrm{(s)}})=

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\mathbf{f},w}

(4.48)

\displaystyle w\notin U

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{gate}}_{L}(\{w,w^{\mathrm{(s)}}\})=

\displaystyle{\mathsf{rdec}}^{\text{out}}_{w}\circ\left(\mathcal{T}_{w,1}^{\text{out}}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathcal{T}_{w,{m_{w}}}^{\text{out}}\right)\circ\widetilde{\mathsf{gate}}_{\kappa}(w)\circ{\mathsf{renc}}^{\text{in}}_{w}

(4.49)

Where we use the abuse of notation
\{w,w^{\mathrm{(s)}}\}
to refer to the vertex under the vertex contraction of the fault.
This is a rearrangement of
C_{\kappa}^{(3)}
, so

\displaystyle\mathsf{map}[C_{L,\mathcal{E}}[\mathbf{f}^{\prime}]]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{map}[C_{\kappa}^{(3)}]=\mathsf{map}[C_{\kappa}^{(2)}]=\mathsf{map}[C_{\kappa}^{(1)}]=\mathsf{map}[C_{\kappa}[\mathbf{f}]]~.\qed

(4.50)

Figure 5:
Pictoral transformation from
C_{\kappa}[\mathbf{f}]
to
C_{L,\Omega}[\mathbf{f}^{\prime}]
. The set of faulty gadgets
U
is highlighted magenta. Every vertex (not in
U
) in the transformed circuit (lower) is paired with an environment vertex. For clarity, connections between environment and computational vertex pairs are drawn as doubled arrows. Green highlighted connections correspond to “pushing” a filter map
\mathcal{R}_{i}
to part of the fault on the previous vertex (see item 1 of case 1).

4.4
Miscellaneous tools

We now include two miscellaneous tools that are generally useful.
The first is the decomposition of faults into Pauli faults.

{lemma}
[Decomposition of faults]
For any circuit
C
and fault
\mathbf{f}
, there exists a new circuit with environment
C_{\mathsf{env}}
and set of Pauli faults
\{\mathbf{f}^{\prime}_{i}\}_{i}
such that:

•

C_{\mathsf{env}}
has computational circuit
C
and a closed environment circuit
C_{\Omega}
(see
Section

3.2
).

•

The map of
C[\mathbf{f}]
is a linear combination of maps of
C_{\mathsf{env}}
subject to the faults
\mathbf{f}_{i}^{\prime}
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{map}[C[\mathbf{f}]]=\sum_{i}\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{env}}[\mathbf{f}^{\prime}_{i}]]

(4.51)

Note that since
C_{\Omega}
is closed, every
\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{env}}[\mathbf{f}^{\prime}_{i}]]
is a superoperator with the same input and output type as
C
and
C[\mathbf{f}]
.

•

Furthermore, for each
i
, the support of each fault
\mathbf{f}^{\prime}_{i}
on the computational circuit is a subset of the support of the original fault
\mathbf{f}
.

Proof.

Note that general fault could replace gates in a circuit, while Pauli faults (
Section

3.2
) are inserted before and after the intended gates. Therefore, the main argument for this proposition is to construct an environment circuit to help rewrite general faults on the computational circuit into Pauli faults.

Denote the circuit as
C=(V,E,\mathsf{conn},\mathsf{type},\mathsf{gate})
, and let the fault be
\mathbf{f}=(\kappa,\mathsf{gate}_{\kappa})
, where
\kappa:V\rightarrow V_{\kappa}
is a contraction map and
\mathsf{gate}_{\kappa}(v)
for
v\in V_{\kappa}
are faulty gates.
We construct
C_{\mathsf{env}}
as follows.
Create two independent copies of
C
, namely
C_{1},C_{2}
with corresponding data. Apply the fault
\mathbf{f}
to
C_{2}
, by taking
\kappa_{2}:V_{2}\rightarrow V_{2,\kappa}
and
\mathsf{gate}_{2,\kappa}
to be a labeling on
V_{2,\kappa}
.
Then
C_{\mathsf{env}}
is our circuit with environment, where
C_{1}
is the computational circuit and
C_{2,\kappa}=(V_{2,\kappa},E_{2,\kappa},\mathsf{conn}_{2,\kappa},\mathsf{type}_{2,\kappa},\mathsf{gate}_{2,\kappa})
is the environment circuit.

To satisfy
Eq.

4.51
, we add, as a new fault
\mathbf{f}^{\prime}
, a collection of
\mathsf{SWAP}
gates between
C_{1}
and
C_{2,\kappa}
, and later decompose these
\mathsf{SWAP}
gates into Pauli faults.
We define the new fault
\mathbf{f}^{\prime}=(\tau,\mathsf{gate}_{\tau})
as follows.
For a vertex
v\in V
, denote its copies in
V_{1},V_{2}
as
v[1],v[2]
, respectively.
Precisely, for every
v\in V
such that
\mathsf{gate}_{2,\kappa}(\kappa(v[2]))\neq\mathsf{gate}_{2}(v[2])
, the set of vertices contracted to
\kappa(v[2])
is
\kappa^{-1}(\kappa(v[2]))
, and the corresponding vertices in
V_{1}
is

\displaystyle T_{v}:=\{u[1]:u[2]\in\kappa^{-1}(\kappa(v[2]))\}.

(4.52)

Define

\displaystyle\text{in}(T_{v})=\bigcup_{u[1]\in T_{v}}\text{in}(u[1]),

(4.53)

and similarly define
\text{out}(T_{v})
.
There is a natural bijection
\phi_{\text{in}}:\text{in}(T_{v})\rightarrow\text{in}(\kappa(v[2]))
, induced by the bijection between
T_{v}
and the vertices contracted to
\kappa(v[2])
.
Similarly there is a natural bijection

\phi_{\text{out}}:\text{out}(T_{v})\rightarrow\text{out}(\kappa(v[2]))
.
We define
\tau
to contract
\kappa(v[2])
and
T_{v}
, and define the gate on this contracted vertex to be

\displaystyle\mathsf{gate}_{\tau}(\tau\left(T_{v}\cup\{\kappa(v[2])\}\right))=

(4.54)

\displaystyle\left(\bigotimes_{e\in\text{out}(T_{v})}\mathsf{SWAP}[e,\phi_{\text{out}}(e)]\right)\circ\left(\mathsf{gate}_{2,\kappa}(\kappa(v[2]))\otimes\bigotimes_{u[1]\in T_{v}}\mathsf{gate}_{1}(u[1])\right)\circ\left(\bigotimes_{e\in\text{in}(T_{v})}\mathsf{SWAP}[e,\phi_{\text{in}}(e)]\right)

(4.55)

This gate is conceptually simple: we
\mathsf{SWAP}
all input edges of vertices in
T_{v}
with input edges of
\kappa(v[2])
, perform the same gates as in
C_{\mathsf{env}}
(which performs a faulty gate on
\kappa(v[2])
as specified by
\mathbf{f}
, and performs the ideal circuit gates as in
C
), and then
\mathsf{SWAP}
the output edges again.
This completes our description of
\mathbf{f}^{\prime}=(\tau,\mathsf{gate}_{\tau})
.

Now observe that the computational circuit of
C_{\mathsf{env}}
is a copy of
C
, while the environment circuit
C_{\Omega}
is a copy of
C[\mathbf{f}]
.
We finish the construction of
C_{\mathsf{env}}
by initializing
27
27

27

This choice of initialization is completely arbitrary.
all input edges of
C_{\Omega}
to
\ket{0}
and tracing out all output edges of
C_{\Omega}
, thereby closing
C_{\Omega}
.
By adding the new fault
\mathbf{f}^{\prime}
, we have
\mathsf{SWAP}
ed the faulty gates from the environment circuit into our computational circuit.
This gives us

\displaystyle\mathsf{map}[C[\mathbf{f}]]=\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{env}}[\mathbf{f}^{\prime}]].

(4.56)

We observe that the fault path of
\mathbf{f}^{\prime}
on the computational circuit of
C_{\mathsf{env}}
is in bijection with the fault path of
\mathbf{f}
on
C
.
Moreover, the fault
\mathbf{f}^{\prime}
conjugates the existing gates in
C_{\mathsf{env}}
by
\mathsf{SWAP}
gates, which can be decomposed into non-diagonal Pauli faults.
This proves our claim.
∎

We will also use the following lemma, which is a statement of the standard fact that stabilizer measurements decoheres general error into Pauli errors.
A proof was given in
[
NP24
]
, we include it here for completeness.

{definition}
[Recoverable Set]
For a stabilizer code
{\mathcal{Q}}
on
n
physical qubits, a subset of qubits
S\subseteq[n]
is
recoverable
if there are no logical operator of
{\mathcal{Q}}
which is fully supported in
S
.

{lemma}
[Decoherence of errors
[
NP24
]
]
For a
n
-qubit stabilizer code
{\mathcal{Q}}
, let
\mathcal{M}
be the channel that measures the
r
stabilizer checks of
{\mathcal{Q}}
and outputs the measurement outcomes.
For a superoperator
\mathcal{E}_{B}
supported on a recoverable subset of qubits

B\subseteq[n]
and a codestate
\rho
of
{\mathcal{Q}}
, the application of the noise superoperator followed by measurement of the checks can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\circ\mathcal{E}_{B}(\rho)=\sum_{s\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}}\alpha_{s}\outerproduct{s}{s}\otimes E_{s}\rho E_{s},

(4.57)

where each
E_{s}
is a Pauli operator supported on
B
and the
\alpha_{s}
are complex coefficients.
When
\mathcal{E}_{B}
is a physical noise channel, they satisfy
\sum_{s}\alpha_{s}=1
.
In other words, measurement of the checks collapses the error into a single Pauli error
as long as we remember the measurement outcome
.

Proof.

Let
\{S_{i}\}_{i\in[r]}
be a basis of the stabilizer group of
{\mathcal{Q}}
. For a syndrome
s\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
, we define the projector to the syndrome space corresponding to
s
as
\Pi_{s}=\prod_{r}(\frac{1}{2}I+(-1)^{s_{i}}S_{i})
. Then the measurement channel
\mathcal{M}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}}\outerproduct{s}{s}\otimes\Pi_{s}\rho\Pi_{s}.

(4.58)

Consider the Kraus decomposition of
\mathcal{E}_{B}
in terms of Pauli operators
\{K_{\mu}\}_{\mu},\{K_{\nu}^{\prime}\}_{\nu}
supported on
B
and complex coefficients
\{\alpha_{\mu,\nu}\}_{\mu,\nu}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\circ\mathcal{E}_{B}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}}\outerproduct{s}{s}\otimes\Pi_{s}\left(\sum_{\mu,\nu}\alpha_{\mu,\nu}K_{\mu}\rho K_{\nu}^{\prime}\right)\Pi_{s}

(4.59)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}}\outerproduct{s}{s}\otimes\left(\sum_{\mu,\nu}\alpha_{\mu,\nu}\Pi_{s}K_{\mu}\rho K_{\nu}^{\prime}\Pi_{s}\right)

(4.60)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}}\outerproduct{s}{s}\otimes\left(\sum_{\mu,\nu}\alpha_{\mu,\nu}\Pi_{s}K_{\mu}\Pi_{0}\rho\Pi_{0}K_{\nu}^{\prime}\Pi_{s}\right)

(4.61)

We see that the projectors
\Pi_{s}
annihilate all terms except those where
K_{\mu},K_{\nu}^{\prime}
both have syndrome
s
. Moreover, sicne
B
is a recoverable set, any two Pauli oeprators supported on
B
which has the same syndrome are equivalent up to stabilizers.
Therefore, for every
s\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}
, there exists Pauli operator
E_{s}
supported on
B
which is equivalent up to stabilizers to all
K_{\mu},K_{\nu}^{\prime}
with syndrome
s
.
In other words,
\Pi_{s}E_{s}\Pi_{0}=\Pi_{s}K_{\mu}\Pi_{0}
for such
\mu,\nu
.
Let
\alpha_{s}
be the sum of all
\alpha_{\mu,\nu}
where
K_{\mu},K_{\nu}^{\prime}
have syndrome
s
.
We can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\circ\mathcal{E}_{B}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}}\outerproduct{s}{s}\otimes\left(\sum_{\mu,\nu}\alpha_{\mu,\nu}\Pi_{s}K_{\mu}\Pi_{0}\rho\Pi_{0}K_{\nu}^{\prime}\Pi_{s}\right)

(4.62)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}}\alpha_{s}\outerproduct{s}{s}\otimes\Pi_{s}E_{s}\Pi_{0}\rho\Pi_{0}E_{s}\Pi_{s}

(4.63)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}}\alpha_{s}\outerproduct{s}{s}\otimes E_{s}\rho E_{s}.\qed

(4.64)

5
Circuit correctness

Here we prove that under various noise models, the total variation distance between the output distribution of the simulated circuit and that of the fault-tolerant circuit can be bounded in terms of the evaluation of the weight enumerator for various noise models.

In what follows, let
C
be a classical-quantum circuit with classical input and classical output.
Note that
\mathsf{map}[C]
is a map from bitstrings to distributions.

Let
C_{\mathsf{FT}}=(V,E,\mathsf{conn},\mathsf{type},\mathsf{gate})
be a gadget for
C
with trivial input and output types and bad fault paths
\mathcal{F}
(e.g. obtained from
Section

4.3
with
\mathcal{W}=\{\{v\}\mid v\in V\}
).
In what follows, in order to obtain standard threshold results, think of
C_{\mathsf{FT}}
as being an element of a family indexed by some element
n
such that below some critical value
x\in[0,\epsilon_{*})
, the evaluation of the weight enumerator can be upper bounded e.g.
\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F};~x\right)}\leq e^{-f(n)}
for some positive function
f
.
Thus, a “universal” threshold theorem simply provides such a family of mappings from circuits to gadgets with upper bounds on the weight enumerators.

5.1
Adversarial noise

Our first noise model is simply an adversarial one.
In this model, an arbitrarily powerful adversary is permitted an arbitrarily large quantum memory and access to qubits supported on a set of locations of the circuit
S\subseteq V
that is
\mathcal{F}
-avoiding.
The adversary is permitted to replace each fault location with an arbitrary channel of the same type.
This is far stronger than the capability to insert Pauli operators: They may, for example, “steal” qubits and insert them into the circuit at a later time.

Let
C_{\mathsf{FT},\Omega}[\mathbf{f}]
be the execution of the circuit
C_{\mathsf{FT}}
in the presence of the adversary.
Their interaction is captured by the introduction of an environment circuit and an
\mathcal{F}
-avoiding
\mathbf{f}
.

{proposition}

Let
C_{\mathsf{FT},\Omega}[\mathbf{f}]
be the execution of
C_{\mathsf{FT}}
in the presence of an arbitrary, closed environment circuit
C_{\Omega}
and a
\mathcal{F}
-avoiding physical fault
\mathbf{f}
, such that
\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{FT},\Omega}[\mathbf{f}]]
is a physical CPTP channel.
It holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{FT},\Omega}[\mathbf{f}]]=\mathsf{map}[C].

(5.1)

Proof.

We first apply
Section

4.4
to write the map of the original circuit in terms of a sum over executions of circuit
C_{\mathsf{FT}}
with a modified environment subject to Pauli faults
\{\mathbf{f}_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i}
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{FT},\Omega}[\mathbf{f}]]=\sum_{i}\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{FT},\Omega^{\prime}}[\mathbf{f}_{i}^{\prime}]].

(5.2)

The Pauli faults are also
\mathcal{F}
-avoiding, so we can apply the definition of a fault-tolerant gadget (simulation property,
Section

4.1
).
For some complex coefficients
\{c_{i}\}_{i}
we can write the following.

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{FT},\Omega^{\prime}}[\mathbf{f}_{i}^{\prime}]]=\sum_{i}c_{i}\mathsf{map}[C]=\mathsf{map}[C].

(5.3)

Where the last equality holds because
\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{FT},\Omega}[\mathbf{f}]]
is a physical (CPTP) channel (which is the case, for example, when each (faulty) gate in
C_{\mathsf{FT},\Omega}[\mathbf{f}]
is physical).
∎

5.2
Local stochastic noise

Our next noise model is related to the adversarial stochastic noise mode of
[
AGP05
]
.
It is the adversarial noise model of the previous section, but the support of the adversary is permitted to be a random variable that obeys a “locally stochastic” property.

{proposition}

Let
C_{\mathsf{FT},\Omega}[\mathbf{f}]
be the execution of
C_{\mathsf{FT}}
in the presence of an arbitrary, closed environment circuit and a physical fault
\mathbf{f}
distributed according to some distribution
M
such that, for all subsets
S\subseteq V
of locations of the computational circuit,
\Pr_{M}(S\subseteq\operatorname{supp}\mathbf{f})\leq\epsilon^{|S|}
.
Then, for any input bitstring
x
, the total variation distance of the output distributions (equivalently, trace distance of the corresponding density operators) is at most
\epsilon_{L}=\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F};~\epsilon\right)}
.
That is, denoting the probability density of
\mathbf{f}
by
P_{M}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tr\left|\mathsf{map}[C]\left(\outerproduct{x}{x}\right)-\left(\sum_{\mathbf{f}}P_{M}(\mathbf{f})\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{FT},\Omega}[\mathbf{f}]](\outerproduct{x}{x})\right)\right|\leq\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F};~\epsilon\right)}.

(5.4)

Proof.

We first compute the probability that the support of
\mathbf{f}
is not
\mathcal{F}
-avoiding.

\displaystyle\Pr_{M}(\operatorname{supp}\mathbf{f}\text{ is not $\mathcal{F}$-avoiding})\leq\sum_{S\in\mathcal{F}}\Pr_{M}(S\subseteq\operatorname{supp}\mathbf{f})\leq\sum_{S\in\mathcal{F}}\epsilon^{|S|}=\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F};~\epsilon\right)}.

(5.5)

Let
\bar{E}
be this subset of events.
Whenever the fault is not in the set, the two density matrices are equal by
Section

5.1
.
Otherwise, the difference may be at most
2
by normalization.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tr\left|\mathsf{map}[C]\left(\outerproduct{x}{x}\right)-\left(\sum_{\mathbf{f}}P_{M}(\mathbf{f})\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{FT},\Omega}[\mathbf{f}]](\outerproduct{x}{x})\right)\right|

(5.6)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{f}}P_{M}(\mathbf{f})\tr\left|\mathsf{map}[C]\left(\outerproduct{x}{x}\right)-\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{FT},\Omega}[\mathbf{f}]](\outerproduct{x}{x})\right|

(5.7)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{f}\in\bar{E}}P_{M}(\mathbf{f})\tr\left|\mathsf{map}[C]\left(\outerproduct{x}{x}\right)-\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{FT},\Omega}[\mathbf{f}]](\outerproduct{x}{x})\right|

(5.8)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\mathbf{f}\in\bar{E}}P_{M}(\mathbf{f})\leq\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F};~\epsilon\right)}.\qed

(5.9)

In the above proposition, the statement holds for any input string
x
because we have assumed classical gates in
C_{\mathsf{FT}}
to be noiseless. This assumption can be dropped if we incorporate classical fault-tolerance constructions.

5.3
Coherent noise

We can also bound against coherent noise in much the same way using a standard technique
[
ABO97
,
AGP05
]
. However, a more careful bound on the contribution from locations outside of the fault support is required.

{proposition}

Let
C_{\mathsf{FT}}[\mathbf{f}]
be the execution of
C_{\mathsf{FT}}
subject to a fault
\mathbf{f}
that does not contract any vertices and applies the correct gate of
C_{\mathsf{FT}}
followed by an operation with diamond distance
\leq\epsilon
from the identity.
Additionally, suppose that

1.

C_{\mathsf{FT}}
is a composition of gadgets,

\displaystyle\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{FT}}]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{map}[C_{m}]\circ\mathsf{map}[C_{m-1}]\circ\dots\circ\mathsf{map}[C_{1}],

(5.10)

and each gadget
C_{i}
has bad fault paths
\mathcal{F}_{i}
such that
\mathcal{F}=\boxplus_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{F}_{i}
;

2.

Let
V_{i}
denote the set of locations of
C_{i}
.
There exists such that for each
i\in[m]
, for every
F_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{i}
, it holds that
|V_{i}|\leq\ratio|F_{i}|
.

Set
\eta=\max_{i}\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{i};~2(1+\epsilon)^{\ratio-1}\epsilon\right)}
.
Then, for any input bitstring
x
, the trace distance of the corresponding density operators is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tr\left|\mathsf{map}[C]\left(\outerproduct{x}{x}\right)-\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{FT}}[\mathbf{f}]](\outerproduct{x}{x})\right|\leq m(1+\eta)^{m}\eta.

(5.11)

Proof.

Since the faults do not contract any locations and act on each gate independently, we can decompose the fault
\mathbf{f}
into
\mathbf{f}_{1},\dots\mathbf{f}_{m}
supported on the locations in
C_{1},\dots,C_{m}
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{FT}}[\mathbf{f}]]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{map}[C_{m}[\mathbf{f}_{m}]]\circ\dots\circ\mathsf{map}[C_{1}[\mathbf{f}_{1}]]

(5.12)

We first analyze each individual gadget
C_{i}[\mathbf{f}_{i}]
.
Let
\widetilde{\mathsf{gate}}_{i}
be the gate map of
\mathbf{f}_{i}
. For each
v\in V_{i}
, we can decompose

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{gate}}_{i}(v)=(1-\epsilon)\mathsf{gate}_{i}(v)+2\epsilon\mathcal{N}_{i}(v),\qquad\text{where }\|\mathcal{N}_{i}(v)\|_{\diamond}\leq 1.

(5.13)

For a subset
S\subseteq V_{i}
, let
\widetilde{\mathsf{gate}}_{i,S}
denote the gate map that takes the difference of the noisy and perfect gate whenever
v\in S
.

\displaystyle v\in V_{i}\qquad\widetilde{\mathsf{gate}}_{i,S}(v)=\begin{cases}2\epsilon\mathcal{N}_{i}(v)&v\in S\\
(1-\epsilon)\mathsf{gate}_{i}(v)&v\notin S\end{cases}.

(5.14)

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{f}}_{i,S}
denote a fault with this gate map.
Using the sub-multiplicativity of the diamond norm, we note that

\displaystyle\|\mathsf{map}[C_{i}[\tilde{\mathbf{f}}_{i,S}]]\|_{\diamond}\leq(2\epsilon)^{|S|}(1-\epsilon)^{|V_{i}|-|S|}

(5.15)

since
\mathsf{map}[C_{i}[\tilde{\mathbf{f}}_{i,S}]]
contains
|S|
operators each with diamond norm at most
2\epsilon
and the remaining operators each have diamond norm
1-\epsilon
.
For simplicity of notation, let us call a set
S
good if it is
\mathcal{F}_{i}
-avoiding and bad otherwise.
We can rewrite the execution of the noisy circuit in terms of these faults:

\displaystyle\mathsf{map}[C_{i}[\mathbf{f}_{i}]]

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\subseteq V_{i}}\mathsf{map}[C_{i}[\tilde{\mathbf{f}}_{i,S}]]

(5.16)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\text{good }S\subseteq V_{i}}\mathsf{map}[C_{i}[\tilde{\mathbf{f}}_{i,S}]]+\sum_{\text{bad }S\subseteq V_{i}}\mathsf{map}[C_{i}[\tilde{\mathbf{f}}_{i,S}]].

(5.17)

Let us denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{i,+}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\text{good }S\subseteq V_{i}}\mathsf{map}[C_{i}[\tilde{\mathbf{f}}_{i,S}]],\qquad\mathcal{C}_{i,-}=\sum_{\text{bad }S\subseteq V_{i}}\mathsf{map}[C_{i}[\tilde{\mathbf{f}}_{i,S}]].

(5.18)

We can bound the total contribution of the ‘bad’ fault paths within a single gadget in terms of diamond norm.

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{C}_{i,-}\right\|_{\diamond}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{bad }S\subseteq V_{i}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{map}[C_{i}[\tilde{\mathbf{f}}_{i,S}]]\right\|_{\diamond}

(5.19)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{i}}\sum_{F\subseteq S\subseteq V_{i}}(2\epsilon)^{|F|}(2\epsilon)^{|S|-|F|}(1-\epsilon)^{|V_{i}|-|S|}

(5.20)

\displaystyle=\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{i}}(2\epsilon)^{|F|}(1+\epsilon)^{|V_{i}|-|F|}

(5.21)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{i}}(2(1+\epsilon)^{\ratio-1}\epsilon)^{|F|}

(5.22)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{i};~2(1+\epsilon)^{\ratio-1}\epsilon\right)}.

(5.23)

Set
\eta=\max_{i}\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{i};~2(1+\epsilon)^{\ratio-1}\epsilon\right)}
.
By the triangular inequality of diamond norm, we see that
\left\|\mathcal{C}_{i,+}\right\|_{\diamond}\leq 1+\eta
for all
i
.
Using these upper bounds, we can bound the total contribution of the bad fault paths on the whole circuit.

\displaystyle\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{FT}}[\mathbf{f}]]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{map}[C_{m}[\mathbf{f}_{m}]]\circ\dots\circ\mathsf{map}[C_{1}[\mathbf{f}_{1}]]

(5.24)

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{C}_{m,+}+\mathcal{C}_{m,-})\circ\dots\circ(\mathcal{C}_{1,+}+\mathcal{C}_{1,-}).

(5.25)

Denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{+}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{m,+}\circ\mathcal{C}_{m-1,+}\circ\dots\mathcal{C}_{1,+},\qquad\mathcal{C}_{-}=\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{FT}}[\mathbf{f}]]-C_{+}.

(5.26)

\mathcal{C}_{-}
has a decomposition based on the last gadget that failed, as follows.

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{-}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}_{m,+}\circ\mathcal{C}_{m-1,+}\circ\dots\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,-}\circ(\mathcal{C}_{i-1,+}+\mathcal{C}_{i-1,-})\circ\dots\circ(\mathcal{C}_{1,+}+\mathcal{C}_{1,-}).

(5.27)

This decomposition enables us to bound the diamond norm of
\mathcal{C}_{-}
. By the sub-multiplicativity of the diamond norm, and the fact that
\mathcal{C}_{i,+}+\mathcal{C}_{i,-}
has diamond norm
1
for all
i
,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{C}_{-}\right\|_{\diamond}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left\|\mathcal{C}_{m,+}\circ\mathcal{C}_{m-1,+}\circ\dots\circ\mathcal{C}_{i,-}\circ(\mathcal{C}_{i-1,+}+\mathcal{C}_{i-1,-})\circ\dots\circ(\mathcal{C}_{1,+}+\mathcal{C}_{1,-})\right\|_{\diamond}

(5.28)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}(1+\eta)^{m-i}\eta

(5.29)

\displaystyle\leq m(1+\eta)^{m}\eta.

(5.30)

On the other hand, since
\mathcal{C}_{+}
is the sum over noisy executions of
C_{\mathsf{FT}}
with
\mathcal{F}
-avoiding faults, it holds by the definition of gadget that

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{+}

\displaystyle=\alpha\cdot\mathsf{map}[C].

(5.31)

for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
. Now fix an input state
\outerproduct{x}{x}
, we write

\displaystyle\tr\left|\mathsf{map}[C]\left(\outerproduct{x}{x}\right)-\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{FT}}[\mathbf{f}]](\outerproduct{x}{x})\right|

(5.32)

\displaystyle=\tr\left|\mathsf{map}[C]\left(\outerproduct{x}{x}\right)-(\mathcal{C}_{+}+\mathcal{C}_{-})(\outerproduct{x}{x})\right|

(5.33)

\displaystyle\leq\tr\left|\mathsf{map}[C]\left(\outerproduct{x}{x}\right)-\alpha\cdot\mathsf{map}[C]\left(\outerproduct{x}{x}\right)\right|+\tr\left|\mathcal{C}_{-}\outerproduct{x}{x}\right|

(5.34)

\displaystyle\leq|1-\alpha|+\|C_{-}\|_{\diamond}

(5.35)

\displaystyle\leq|1-\alpha|+m(1+\eta)^{m}\eta,

(5.36)

where we used here that the trace norm is bounded by the diamond norm.
To bound
|1-\alpha|
, note that

\displaystyle 1=\tr(\circuitMap{\cft[\fault]}(\ketbra{x}{x}))

\displaystyle=\tr(C_+\ketbra{x}{x})+\tr(C_-\ketbra{x}{x})=\alpha+\tr(C_-\ketbra{x}{x}).

(5.37)

Therefore we can bound

\displaystyle|1-\alpha|

\displaystyle=|\tr(C_-\ketbra{x}{x})|\leq\tr\|C_{-}\outerproduct{x}{x}\|\leq\|C_{-}\outerproduct{x}{x}\|_{\diamond}=m(1+\eta)^{m}\eta.

(5.38)

We conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tr\left|\mathsf{map}[C]\left(\outerproduct{x}{x}\right)-\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{FT}}[\mathbf{f}]](\outerproduct{x}{x})\right|

\displaystyle=m(1+\eta)^{m}\eta.\qed

(5.39)

6
Application: qLDPC codes and transversal gates

We show that a standard construction of error correction gadgets (originating from
[
Den+02
,
Got14
]
) for quantum-low density parity check (qLDPC) codes satisfies our gadget definition.

6.1
Correctable sets

A CSS code defined by the check matrices
(H_{X},H_{Z})
is said to be
\Delta
-qLDPC if every row and column of
H_{X}
and
H_{Z}
has weight at most
\Delta
.

For an error rate
p
(IID) on
n
qubits, on average, errors have weight around
np
.
Thus, naively one might conclude that linear distance scaling is a necessary condition for a code family to achieve a threshold.
This turns out to be too strong: It suffices to be able to correct “most” errors of weight
np
.
It turns out that the qLDPC property imposes strong constraints
28
28

28

More specifically, the qLDPC property induces a sort of “geometry” on the space of qubits, and logical operators must be the union of large connected components.
on the low weight logical operators of the code, so that it is rare for a random error to have large overlap with a logical operator.

[
KP13
]
showed that all qLDPC code families with polynomial distance scaling posses a threshold – below a critical error rate, recovery from depolarizing noise fails with exponentially small (in the code distance) probability when decoding with a minimum weight decoder.

We begin by defining a minimum-weight decoder for a classical linear code as well as a minimum-weight decoder in the
X/Z
basis which we refer to as the CSS minimum-weight decoder.

{definition}
[Minimum-weight decoder]
For a classical code defined by a
m\times n
parity check matrix
H
, given a syndrome
s\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}
in the column space of
H
, a minimum weight decoder outputs a minimum Hamming weight vector in the set of valid corrections,
\{x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}:Hx=s\}
.
For a CSS code defined by
(H_{X},H_{Z})
, a CSS minimum weight decoder
D\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}\mapsto\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{n}
consists of two minimum weight decoders,
D_{X},D_{Z}
, for matices
H_{X},H_{Z}
.
For a syndrome
s=(s_{X},s_{Z})
, where
s_{X},s_{Z}
are the
X
and
Z
syndromes respectively,

\displaystyle D(s)=X^{D_{Z}(s_{Z})}\cdot Z^{D_{X}(s_{X})}.

(6.1)

Here for a vector
v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
, we use
X^{v}
to denote the Pauli operator
X^{v_{1}}\otimes\dots X^{v_{n}}
, where
X^{1}=X
and
X^{0}=I
.
Z^{v}
is defined similarly.

We now need a parameterized notion of bad sets (from
[
Got14
]
) of the code.
These are the sets that will trick our decoder into applying the wrong correction.

{definition}
[Adjacency graph]
For a check matrix
H\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r\times n}
, the adjacency graph of
H
, denoted
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H]
is a graph on the vertices
[n]
, where two vertices
i\neq j\in[n]
are adjacent if there exists a row
k
which is non-zero on columns
i
and
j
. I.e. if bit vertices
i
and
j
are connected by a constraint vertex
k
in the Tanner graph associated with
H
.

We will now associate length-
n
bitstrings with subsets of
[n]
.

{proposition}
[
[
KP13
]
]
For a pair of check matrices
(H_{X},H_{Z})
defining a CSS code of distance
d
, any non-trivial
X
logical operator with support
x
must induce a subgraph of
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{Z}]
with at least one connected component of size at least
d
.

{definition}

[Clustering sets]
For a graph
G=(V,E)
the
(d,t)
-clustering sets
\mathcal{CG}_{(d,t)}\subseteq P(V)
of
G
are the subsets
W\subseteq U\subseteq V
of
V
of size
|W|=t
contained in a subset
U
of size
|U|=d
that induces a connected subgraph of
G
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{CG}_{(d,d)}

\displaystyle=\left\{U\subseteq V\mid|U|=d\text{ and $U$ induces a connected subgraph of $G$}\right\}

(6.2)

\displaystyle t<d\quad\mathcal{CG}_{(d,t)}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{U\in\mathcal{CG}_{(d,d)}}\left\{W\subseteq U\mid|W|=t\right\}

(6.3)

The
t
parameter of these clusters is the appropriate analog of the weight of an error for qLDPC code families with sublinear distance: The error cannot be too high weight in any one region.
As we will see, this parameter will behave much like Hamming weight in that we can recovery a sort of sub-additivity, see
Section

6.1
.

We give upper bounds on the weight enumerator of the clustering sets by bounding the number of bad sets analogously to
[
Got14
]
.
We first need the following lemma from
[
AGP07
]
.
29
29

29

We actually require the stronger variant proven (but not stated) in
[
AGP07
]
. See Lemma 2 of
[
Got14
]
.

{lemma}
[Cluster counting
[
AGP07
]
Lemma 5]
Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph with maximum degree
\Delta
.
Fix a subset
S\subseteq V
of vertices.
Then, consider subsets
C\subseteq V
that is a union of connected sets
C=\cup_{i}C_{i}
in
G
where each set
C_{i}
contains at least one element of
S
,
C_{i}\cap S\neq\varnothing
.
We say that
C
is a
cluster cover
of
S
, and denote the collection of cluster covers of size
m
to be
\mathcal{CO}(m,S)
.
The number of such subsets of size
m
,
M(m,S)
satisfies the upper bound

\displaystyle M(m,S)\leq e^{|S|-1}(\Delta e)^{m-|S|}

(6.4)

This allows us to upper bound the weight enumerator of this family.

{lemma}

For a maximum degree-
\Delta
graph
G=(V,E)
with the
(d,t)
-clustering sets
\mathcal{CG}_{(d,t)}\subseteq P(V)
, we have that (
x\in[0,1]
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{CG}_{(d,t)};~x\right)}\leq\left(\binom{d}{t-1}(\Delta e)^{d-1}|V|\right)x^{t}

(6.5)

Proof.

We compute the number of elements of
\mathcal{CG}_{(d,t)}
by taking a union bound over the following decomposition:

\displaystyle\mathcal{CG}_{(d,t)}\subseteq\bigcup_{v\in V}\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\subseteq V\\
|U|=d\\
\text{$U$ is connected in $G$}\end{subarray}}\left\{W\subseteq U\mid|W|=t\text{ and }v\subseteq W\right\}

(6.6)

Footnote

29
upper bounds the number of connected (in
G
) sets
U\subseteq V
of size
|U|=d
containing a particular vertex
v\in V
as
(\Delta e)^{d-1}
.
There are
\binom{d}{t-1}
subsets of
U
that contain
v
.
By construction, each element of
\mathcal{CG}_{(d,t)}
has weight
t
.
The result follows.
∎

We are now ready to define our code types.
From now on, fix a
\Delta
-qLDPC code
{\mathcal{Q}}
with parameters
\llbracket n,k,d\rrbracket
defined by check matrices
(H_{X},H_{Z})
and an arbitrary choice of irreversible encoding map implementable by a Clifford circuit
M
acting on a logical state
\mathsf{D}\left(\mathcal{H}^{k}\right)
and an
n-k
ancilla register initialized to
\ket{0}^{\otimes(n-k)}
.
Call this the
computational code
.

M
satisfies the property that for some choice of
n-k
generators of the stabilizer
\{s_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n-k}\subseteq\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{n-k}
, any eigenstate in eigenspace
s\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}
of the stabilizer generators (the
syndrome space

s
) can be written as
M\left(\ket{\psi}\otimes\ket{s}\right)
for some state
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}^{k}
.

{definition}

[Purified decoder]
Let
\sigma\colon\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}
be the syndrome map with respect to the generating set of the stabilizer
\{s_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n-k}\subseteq\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{n}
of
{\mathcal{Q}}
and
D\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}\mapsto\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{n}
be a decoding algorithm outputting a correction such that
\sigma\circ D(s)=s
.
We can define the purification
U_{D}
of
D
as the unitary
U
that measures the syndrome
s
, applies the corresponding correction
D(s)
, and places the value of the syndrome in the ancilla register after applying the unencoding circuit
M^{\dagger}
.
Define the syndrome space projector
\Pi_{s}=M\left(I\otimes\outerproduct{s}{s}\right)M^{\dagger}
.
Then
U_{D}
can be written in terms of a sum over syndrome spaces and the corresponding corrections.

\displaystyle U_{D}=\sum_{s\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}}(I\otimes\outerproduct{s}{0})M^{\dagger}D(s)\Pi_{s}

(6.7)

{definition}

[Code types for computational code]
Let
\mathcal{CG}_{(m,t)}^{(X)}
and
\mathcal{CG}_{(m,t)}^{(Z)}
be the
(m,t)
-clustering sets in
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{X}]
and
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{Z}]
, respectively.
Let
c\in[0,1]
.
All the bad error supports will be stated in terms of
c
.
We define the bad error supports to be all sufficiently large subsets of connected subsets of size at least
d
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{(X)}_{c}

\displaystyle=\boxplus^{n}_{m=d}\mathcal{CG}_{(m,\lceil c\cdot m/2\rceil)}^{(X)}

(6.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{(Z)}_{c}

\displaystyle=\boxplus^{n}_{m=d}\mathcal{CG}_{(m,\lceil c\cdot m/2\rceil)}^{(Z)}

(6.9)

We define
\mathcal{B}_{c}\equiv\mathcal{B}^{(X)}_{c}\boxplus\mathcal{B}^{(Z)}_{c}
.

The reversible decoding unitary
U_{{\mathcal{Q}}}
is the purification of a CSS minimum-weight decoder (see
Section

6.1
) for
{\mathcal{Q}}
, as defined in
Eq.

6.7
.
The code type
\mathsf{CompCode}_{c}
is defined to be the pair of bad sets and the purified minimum weight decoder
\left(U_{{\mathcal{Q}}},\mathcal{B}_{c}\equiv\mathcal{B}^{(X)}_{c}\boxplus\mathcal{B}^{(Z)}_{c}\right)
.

Note that
U_{{\mathcal{Q}}}
inverts
M
on the trivial syndrome space and can be easily confirmed to be unitary.

\displaystyle\forall\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}^{k}\quad\ket{\psi}=(U_{{\mathcal{Q}}}M)\ket{\psi}\ket{0}^{\otimes n-k}

(6.10)

We now show that
\mathsf{CompCode}_{c}
is a valid code type
Section

4
.

{proposition}
[Correctable errors
[
KP13
,
Got14
]
]
For
c\in[0,1]
, let
\mathsf{CompCode}_{c}=(U_{{\mathcal{Q}}},\mathcal{B}_{c})
.
Fix a logical state
\rho\in\mathsf{D}\left(\mathcal{H}^{k}\right)
and a state
\sigma\in\mathsf{L}\left(\mathcal{H}^{n}\right)
such that
\sigma
is
\mathcal{B}_{c}
-deviated from the encoding of
\rho
:
\mathsf{enc}(\rho)\precapprox_{\mathcal{B}_{c}}\sigma
.

Then, the decoding of
\sigma
is proportional to
\rho
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{dec}(\sigma)\propto\rho

(6.11)

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a superoperator whose support
S\subseteq[n]
is
\mathcal{B}_{c}
-avoiding.
First, we write the Kraus decomposition of the superoperator in terms of Pauli operators
\{K_{\mu}\}_{\mu},\{K_{\nu}^{\prime}\}_{\nu}
supported on
S\subseteq[n]
and complex coefficients
\{\alpha_{\mu,\nu}\}_{\mu,\nu}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\circ\mathsf{enc}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu,\nu}\alpha_{\mu,\nu}K_{\mu}M(\rho\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0})M^{\dagger}K_{\nu}^{\prime}

(6.12)

Using this decomposition and the definition of
U_{{\mathcal{Q}}}
(
Eq.

6.7
), we consider the action of
\mathsf{dec}
on
\sigma
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{dec}(\sigma)=\tr_{\mathcal{H}^{r}}\left(U_{{\mathcal{Q}}}(\mathcal{E}\circ\mathsf{enc}(\rho))U_{{\mathcal{Q}}}^{\dagger}\right)

(6.13)

\displaystyle=\tr_{\mathcal{H}^{r}}\left(\sum_{s,t\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}}(I\otimes\outerproduct{s}{0})M^{\dagger}D(s)\Pi_{s}\left(\sum_{\mu,\nu}\alpha_{\mu,\nu}K_{\mu}M(\rho\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0})M^{\dagger}K_{\nu}^{\prime}\right)\Pi_{t}D(t)M(I\otimes\outerproduct{0}{t})\right)

(6.14)

\displaystyle=\tr_{\mathcal{H}^{r}}\left(\sum_{s,t\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}}\left(\sum_{\mu,\nu}\alpha_{\mu,\nu}(I\otimes\outerproduct{s}{0})M^{\dagger}D(s)\Pi_{s}K_{\mu}M(\rho\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0})M^{\dagger}K_{\nu}^{\prime}\Pi_{t}D(t)M(I\otimes\outerproduct{0}{t})\right)\right)

(6.15)

Let
s_{\mu},s_{\nu}
denote the syndrome of
K_{\mu},K_{\nu}^{\prime}
.
The projectors
\Pi_{s}
and
\Pi_{t}
annihilate all terms except for those where
s_{\mu}=s
and
s_{\nu}=t
.
Suppose
D(s)K_{\mu}
and
D(t)K_{\nu}^{\prime}
are both stabilizers, we can write

\displaystyle D(s)\Pi_{s}K_{\mu}M(\rho\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0})M^{\dagger}K_{\nu}^{\prime}\Pi_{t}D(t)

\displaystyle=M(\rho\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0})M^{\dagger}.

(6.16)

We can then simplify the equation for
\mathsf{dec}(\sigma)
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{dec}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\tr_{\mathcal{H}^{r}}\left(\sum_{s,t\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}}\left(\sum_{\mu,\nu:s_{\mu}=s,s_{\nu}=t}\alpha_{\mu,\nu}(I\otimes\outerproduct{s}{0})M^{\dagger}M(\rho\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0})M^{\dagger}M(I\otimes\outerproduct{0}{t})\right)\right)

(6.17)

\displaystyle=\tr_{\mathcal{H}^{r}}\left(\sum_{s,t\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}}\left(\sum_{\mu,\nu:s_{\mu}=s,s_{\nu}=t}\alpha_{\mu,\nu}\rho\otimes\outerproduct{s}{t}\right)\right).

(6.18)

The partial trace annihilates all terms where
s\neq r
. Let
\alpha_{s}
be the sum of all
\alpha_{\mu,\nu}
where
s_{\mu}=s_{\nu}=s
. Then the above simplifies to

\displaystyle\mathsf{dec}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n-k}}\alpha_{s}\rho.

(6.19)

It suffices for us to show that for
an arbitrary Pauli error
E
with
\mathcal{B}^{(X)}_{c}\boxplus\mathcal{B}^{(Z)}_{c}
-avoiding support, the minimum weight decoder will correct
E
up to a stabilizer.
Decompose the Pauli error
E=pE_{X}E_{Z}
into
X
and
Z
parts
E_{X}\in\{X,I\}^{n}
and
E_{Z}\in\{Z,I\}^{n}
up to a scalar
p\in\{\pm 1,\pm i\}
.
Consider the correction of
E_{X}
, the case of
E_{Z}
is identical with the roles of
Z
and
X
interchanged.

Since
E
has
\mathcal{B}_{c}
-avoiding support,
E_{X}
must have
\mathcal{B}^{(Z)}_{c}=\boxplus^{n}_{m=d}\mathcal{CG}_{(m,\lceil c\cdot m/2\rceil)}^{(Z)}
-avoiding support.
The minimum weight decoder applies a minimum weight correction
C_{X}\in\{I,X\}^{n}
such that
C_{X}E_{X}
has trivial syndrome.
We will show that
C_{X}E_{X}
is in the stabilizer of the code, following
[
KP13
,
Got14
]
.

We first establish some properties of the corrected error
C_{X}E_{X}
.

\operatorname{supp}(C_{X}E_{X})
induces a subgraph of the adjacency graph
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{Z}]
that has connected cluster
L_{1},\dots,L_{\ell}
.
From the definition of the adjacency graph, each connected cluster must have trivial syndrome (commute with the stabilizer of the code) if
C_{X}E_{X}
does.
Thus, the restriction of
C_{X}E_{X}
to any connected cluster
L^{\prime}
of size strictly less than the code distance
d
must be in the stabilizer of the code.
It also must be the case that on
L^{\prime}
, the error is supported on at least half of the set,
|L^{\prime}\cap\operatorname{supp}E_{X}|\geq|L^{\prime}|/2
.
Otherwise,
E_{X}|_{L^{\prime}}
would be a lower weight correction than
C_{X}|_{L^{\prime}}
for
E_{X}|_{L^{\prime}}
.

Now suppose, for a contradiction, that
C_{X}E_{X}
is not a stabilizer of the code.
Then, at least one connected cluster
L
of
\operatorname{supp}(C_{X}E_{X})
in
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{Z}]
must have size
m\geq d
.
Since
L
satisfies
|L\cap\operatorname{supp}E_{X}|\geq|L|/2
,
\operatorname{supp}E_{X}
contains a subset of
L
of size at least
m/2
.
So this subset this is a superset of an element of
\mathcal{CG}_{(m,\lceil c\cdot m/2\rceil)}^{(Z)}
, contradicting the assumption that
E
was
\mathcal{B}_{c}
-avoiding.

An identical argument holds for
C_{Z}E_{Z}
, so
C_{Z}E_{Z}C_{X}E_{X}
is in the stabilizer of the code and
E
is corrected by the CSS minimum-weight decoder.
This completes our proof.
∎

We will also need to be able to analyze the structure of unions of sets that avoid the clustering sets.

{definition}

[Separable Bad Sets]
For
\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2}\subseteq P(\Omega)
, we say that
\mathcal{B}
is
separable
into
\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2}
if for all
\mathcal{B}_{1}
-avoiding set
X
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}
-avoiding set
Y
,
X\cup Y
is
\mathcal{B}
-avoiding.

{proposition}

[Union of avoiding sets]
Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph, and
\mathcal{CG}_{(d,t)}
be the
(d,t)
-clustering sets of
G
.
For
s,t\in\mathbb{N}
such that
s+t\leq d
,
\mathcal{CG}_{(d,{s+t})}
is separable into
\mathcal{CG}_{(d,{s})}
and
\mathcal{CG}_{(d,{t})}
.

Proof.

Consider
X,Y\subseteq[V]
such that
X
is
\mathcal{CG}_{(d,s)}
-avoiding and
Y
is
\mathcal{CG}_{(d,t)}
-avoiding, let
Z=X\cup Y
.
Suppose, for a contradiction, that
Z
is not
\mathcal{CG}_{(d,{s+t})}
-avoiding.
Then, there exists a subset of vertices
U\subseteq V
of size
|U|=d
such that
U
induces a connected subgraph of
G
and
|U\cap Z|\geq s+t
.

However, since
X
and
Y
are
\mathcal{CG}_{(d,s)}
-avoiding and
\mathcal{CG}_{(d,t)}
-avoiding, respectively, we have that their intersections with
U
must be small:
|U\cap X|<s
and
|U\cap Y|<t
.
The contradiction follows.

\displaystyle s+t\leq|U\cap Z|

\displaystyle\leq|U\cap X|+|U\cap Y|<s+t.\qed

(6.20)

It is a straightforward corollary that if we combine errors (e.g. by applying a two-qubit gate transversally) then the resulting error is well controlled.

{corollary}

For
c+c^{\prime}\leq 1
,
\mathcal{B}_{c+c^{\prime}}
is separable into
\mathcal{B}_{c}
and
\mathcal{B}_{c^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Apply
Section

6.1
to the clustering sets in the definition of
\mathcal{B}_{c}
.
∎

Concluding our definition of the computational code type, we bound the weight enumerator of
\mathcal{B}_{c}
.

{lemma}

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{B}_{c};~x\right)}

\displaystyle\leq n\cdot[O_{\Delta,c}(x)]^{\frac{cd}{2}}.

(6.21)

Proof.

Since
H_{X},H_{Z}
has row and column weights bounded by
\Delta
, the graphs
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{X}],G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{Z}]
has degree bounded by
\Delta^{2}
.
We can bound the weight enumerator of
\mathcal{B}_{c}
with
Section

6.1
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{B}^{(X)}_{c};~x\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m\geq d}\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{CG}^{(X)}_{(m,\lceil c\cdot m/2\rceil)};~x\right)}

(6.22)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m\geq d}n\cdot(2e\Delta^{2})^{m}x^{\frac{cm}{2}}

(6.23)

\displaystyle\leq n\cdot[O_{\Delta,c}(x)]^{\frac{cd}{2}}

(6.24)

Since
\mathcal{B}_{c}=\mathcal{B}^{(X)}_{c}\boxplus\mathcal{B}^{(Z)}_{c}
, we multiply the above upper bound by a factor of 2, which is absorbed into the constants in
O_{\Delta,c}(x)
.
∎

6.2
Spacetime code

Before we are ready to construct and prove properties of the error correction gadget, we first need to construct the following classical code commonly referred to as a “spacetime code.”
In what follows, we will use the CSS property to focus on correction of
X
errors.
The case of
Z
errors will follow with
X
and
Z
interchanged.

{definition}
[Spacetime code]
Let
(H_{X},H_{Z})
be a CSS code.
The spacetime code of
H_{Z}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r_{Z}\times n}
is a modified check matrix that corresponds to the spacetime history of repeated syndrome extraction.
30
30

30

Syndrome bits of this code are sometimes called “detectors.”

The spacetime code check matrix
H\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{(Tr_{Z})\times T(n+r_{Z})}
will have
T
copies of
H_{Z}
along the diagonal and
T
blocks of
r_{Z}
extra columns corresponding to syndrome measurement errors.
Each measurement error will be able to flip the syndrome of a check in two consecutive rounds.

We describe this more explicitly.
In what follows, for two intervals
I
and
J
, we will use
H[I,J]
to denote the sub-matrix with rows in
I
and columns in
J
.
For
t\in[T]
, we define the data error intervals and syndrome error intervals (see
Fig.

6
).

\displaystyle I_{t}

\displaystyle=[(t-1)\cdot r_{Z}+1,~t\cdot r_{Z}-1]

(6.25)

\displaystyle J_{t}^{\mathrm{d}}

\displaystyle=[(t-1)\cdot n+1,~t\cdot n-1]

(6.26)

\displaystyle J_{t}^{\mathrm{s}}

\displaystyle=[(t-1)\cdot r_{Z}+Tn,~t\cdot r_{Z}+Tn-1]

(6.27)

We can now specify the entries of
H
(all unspecified entries are 0).

\displaystyle t\in[T]

\displaystyle H[I_{t},J_{t}^{\mathrm{d}}]

\displaystyle=H_{Z}

(6.28)

\displaystyle t\in[T]

\displaystyle H[I_{t},J_{t}^{\mathrm{s}}]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Id}_{r_{Z}\times r_{Z}}

(6.29)

\displaystyle t\in[T-1]

\displaystyle H[I_{t},J_{t+1}^{\mathrm{s}}]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Id}_{r_{Z}\times r_{Z}}

(6.30)

We will also require an “interpretation” of the results of decoding the spacetime code.
Define the
flattening map

\phi\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{Tn+Tr_{z}}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
given by dropping the syndrome error blocks and summing together the data
error blocks
x\mapsto\sum_{t\in[T]}x[J_{t}^{\mathrm{d}}]
.

Figure 6:
Spacetime code check matrix

The rows
I_{t}
correspond to the change in syndrome measurements in the
t
-th round.
The columns
J_{t}^{\mathrm{s}}
correspond to syndrome measurement errors affecting the syndrome at rounds
t-1
and
t
while the columns
J_{t}^{\mathrm{d}}
correspond to new data errors accumulated in round
t
.

For our error correction gadget, we will consider the
input error
, which corresponds to bits of
J_{1}^{\mathrm{d}}
, separately from the remaining error.
For this purpose, we define the
bulk
of the spacetime code with bits
J_{\mathsf{bulk}}:=[T(n+r_{Z})]\setminus J^{\mathrm{d}}_{1}
and checks
I_{\mathsf{bulk}}=I_{2}\cup\dots\cup I_{T}
namely, all data and syndrome bits except for the input error bits and all rows except for the first.
We denote this
bulk check matrix

H_{\mathsf{bulk}}
.
Similarly, we define the
bulk flatting map

\phi_{\mathsf{bulk}}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}^{(T-1)n+Tr_{z}}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
as
\phi_{\mathsf{bulk}}(x)=\sum_{t=2}^{T}x[J_{t}^{\mathrm{d}}]
.
We call the last
r_{Z}
bits
J_{T}^{\mathrm{s}}
of a bitstring
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{Tn+Tr_{z}}
the
final round syndrome error
bits, and the first
r_{Z}
bits
J_{1}^{\mathrm{s}}
the
initial round syndrome error
.

We will now prove some properties about the spacetime code: Let
d
be the distance of the original CSS code.

{lemma}

[Final round syndrome]
For a bitstring
x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{Tn+Tr_{Z}}
with trivial spacetime syndrome
Hx=0
, the syndrome of the flattened bitstring
\phi(x)
is equal to the final round syndrome error.

\displaystyle H_{Z}\phi(x)=x[J_{T}^{\mathrm{s}}]

(6.31)

Proof.

x
has zero syndrome with respect to the spacetime code
Hx=0
which implies (by definition of
H
) the following system of equations.

\displaystyle t\in[T-1]\quad H_{Z}x[J_{t+1}^{\mathrm{d}}]+x[J_{t}^{\mathrm{s}}]+x[J_{t+1}^{\mathrm{s}}]

\displaystyle=0

(6.32)

\displaystyle H_{Z}x[J_{1}^{\mathrm{d}}]+x[J_{1}^{\mathrm{s}}]

\displaystyle=0

(6.33)

Summing over this linear system yields the result.

\displaystyle\sum_{t\in[t]}H_{Z}x[J_{t}^{\mathrm{d}}]+x[J_{T}^{\mathrm{s}}]

\displaystyle=H_{Z}\phi(x)+x[J_{T}^{\mathrm{s}}]=0.\qed

(6.34)

{lemma}

Similarly, for a bitstring
x_{\mathsf{bulk}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{(T-1)n+Tr_{Z}}\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}^{J_{\mathsf{bulk}}}
with no bulk spacetime syndrome
H_{\mathsf{bulk}}x=0
, its flattened syndrome is the difference between the initial and final round syndrome errors.

\displaystyle H_{Z}\phi_{\mathsf{bulk}}(x_{\mathsf{bulk}})=x_{\mathsf{bulk}}[J_{T}^{\mathrm{s}}]+x_{\mathsf{bulk}}[J_{1}^{\mathrm{s}}]

(6.35)

Proof.

As in the proof of
Section

6.2
, the zero syndrome condition implies the following equation from which the result follows.

\displaystyle H_{Z}\phi_{\mathsf{bulk}}(x_{\mathsf{bulk}})+x_{\mathsf{bulk}}[J_{T}^{\mathrm{s}}]+x_{\mathsf{bulk}}[J_{1}^{\mathrm{s}}]

\displaystyle=0\qed

(6.36)

{lemma}

For a bitstring
x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{Tn+Tr_{Z}}
with trivial spacetime syndrome
Hx=0
and trivial final round syndrome error
x[J_{T}^{\mathrm{s}}]=0
, if
x
is the union of connected components in the adjacency graph of the spacetime code
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H]
of size strictly less than the distance
<d
, then
x
flattens to a trivial logical operator of the CSS code,
\phi(x)\in\rowspan(H_{X})
.

Proof.

Consider a single connected component
x^{\prime}
of
x
in
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H]
.
Since no other row of
H
with support on
x^{\prime}
has support on
x-x^{\prime}
, it must be that
Hx^{\prime}=0
which implies
H_{Z}\phi(x^{\prime})=0
by
Section

6.2
and the assumption on the final round syndrome bits
x[J_{T}^{\mathrm{s}}]=0
.
Using the assumption on the size of each connected component, the Hamming weight of the flattened bitstring is less than the distance
|\phi(x^{\prime})|\leq|x^{\prime}|<d
.

\phi(x^{\prime})
is in the kernel of
H_{Z}
and has Hamming weight less than
d
, so by the distance of the CSS code
(H_{X},H_{Z})
, it must be in the rowspace of
H_{X}
,
\phi(x^{\prime})\in\rowspan(H_{X})
.
It then follows that every connected component of
x
flattens to an element of the row space of
H_{X}
.

\phi(x)
is the sum over the flattening of every connected component so it is also in the row space of
H_{X}
.
∎

{definition}

[Extended spacetime decoding graph]
To bound the residual error on the code after correction, we introduce a slight modification to
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H]
.
We expand
H
by
n
columns and
r_{Z}
rows, which we denote
J_{T+1}^{\mathrm{d}}
and
I_{T+1}
.
We denote this new matrix
H_{\mathsf{ext}}
with the additional entries

\displaystyle H_{\mathsf{ext}}[I_{T+1},J_{T+1}^{\mathrm{d}}]

\displaystyle=H_{Z},\qquad H_{\mathsf{ext}}[I_{T+1},J_{T}^{\mathrm{s}}]=\mathsf{Id}_{r_{Z}\times r_{Z}}~.

(6.37)

The graph
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{\mathsf{ext}}]
, compared to
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H]
, has one more layer of
n
vertices labeled by
J_{T+1}^{\mathrm{d}}
which are connected to vertices labeled by
J_{T}^{\mathrm{s}}
.
We say that
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{\mathsf{ext}}]
is the
extended spacetime decoding graph
.

{lemma}

[Residual Error
[
Got14
]
]
For a bitstring
x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{Tn+Tr_{Z}}
with trivial spacetime syndrome
Hx=0
,
let
y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
be a minimum weight vector (supported on
J_{T+1}^{\mathrm{d}}
) such that
H_{Z}\phi(x)=H_{Z}y
.
Let
S\subseteq J_{T+1}^{\mathrm{d}}
be a set.
If
y
contains
S
, then there must exist a cluster cover
R
of
S
(see
Footnote

29
),
R\subseteq x\sqcup y
, such that
|R|\geq 2|S|
and for every cluster
C
of
R
, we have
|x\cap C|\geq|C|/2
.

Proof.

Consider the set of vertices
x\sqcup y
.
Let
x^{\prime}\sqcup y^{\prime}
denote the restriction of
x\sqcup y
to maximal connected clusters in
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{\mathsf{ext}}]
containing vertices in
S
, with
x^{\prime}\subseteq x,y^{\prime}\subseteq y
. Note that
S\subseteq y^{\prime}\subseteq y
and
R=x^{\prime}\sqcup y^{\prime}
is a cluster cover of
S
in
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{\mathsf{ext}}]
.
By
Section

6.2
,
for every cluster
C
in
R
,
we have
H_{Z}(y^{\prime}|_{C})=H_{Z}\phi(x^{\prime}|_{C})
.
Moreover, since
y
is the minimum weight vector with
H_{Z}y=H_{Z}\phi(x)
, we have that
(y^{\prime}|_{C})
is the minimum weight vector with
H_{Z}(y^{\prime}|_{C})=H_{Z}\phi(x^{\prime}|_{C})
.
Therefore

\displaystyle|x\cap C|=|x^{\prime}|_{C}|\geq|\phi(x^{\prime}|_{C})|\geq|y^{\prime}|_{C}|=|C\cap S|,

(6.38)

which implies that
2|x\cap C|\geq|x^{\prime}|_{C}|+|y^{\prime}|_{C}|=|C|
. Our claim follows.
∎

{lemma}

For a bitstring
x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{Tn+Tr_{Z}}
with trivial spacetime syndrome
Hx=0
,
let
y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
be a minimum weight vector (supported on
J_{T+1}^{\mathrm{d}}
) such that
H_{Z}\phi(x)=H_{Z}y
.
If
\phi(x)-y\notin\rowspan(H_{X})
, then there must exists a connected cluster of vertices
S
of size at least
d
in
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{\mathsf{ext}}]
such that
|x\cap S|\geq|S|/2
.

Proof.

Since
\phi(x)-y\notin\rowspan(H_{X})
, it must be the case that
\phi(x)-y
contains a connected cluster of size at least
d
in
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{Z}]
, the adjacency matrix of the base code
H_{Z}
.
This implies that
x\sqcup y
contains a connected cluster
S
of size at least
d
in
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{\mathsf{ext}}]
.
Restricting to this cluster
S
, we see that
H_{Z}y|_{S}=H_{Z}\phi(x|_{S})
, and
y|_{S}
is the minimum weight vector satisfying this equality. Therefore we have

\displaystyle|x\cap S|=|x|_{S}|\geq|\phi(x|_{S})|\geq|y|_{S}|.

(6.39)

This implies that
|x\cap S|\geq|S|/2
, as desired.
∎

We are now ready to prove when a correction procedure for the spacetime code succeeds.

{definition}

[Decoding]
Consider the extended spacetime decoding graph
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{\mathsf{ext}}]
.
Let
e_{\text{in}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{|J^{\mathrm{d}}_{1}|}
be an input error, and
e\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{|J_{\mathsf{bulk}}|}
be an error on the bulk.
We denote
\tilde{e}=e_{\text{in}}\sqcup e
.
Let
c\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{|J_{\mathsf{bulk}}|}
be the minimum weight vector such that
H(e)=H(c)
, and let
c_{\text{in}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}^{|J^{\mathrm{d}}_{1}|}
be the minimum weight vector such that

\displaystyle H_{Z}(c_{\text{in}})=H_{Z}(e_{\text{in}})+(e+c)|_{J^{\mathrm{s}}_{1}}.

(6.40)

Denote
\tilde{c}=c_{\text{in}}\sqcup c
, then
\tilde{c}
is the output correction of this minimum-weight decoding procedure, with
H(\tilde{e}+\tilde{c})=0
.
Let
y\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{|J^{\mathrm{d}}_{T+1}|}
be the minimum weight vector that satisfies

\displaystyle H_{Z}\phi(\tilde{e}+\tilde{c})=H_{Z}y,

(6.41)

we say that
y
is the
residue error
on the code block after decoding.

{lemma}

[Successful Decoding]
Recall the code type and bad error supports defined in
Section

6.1
.
Following the definitions and notations in
Section

6.2
, let
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}\subseteq P(J^{\mathrm{d}}_{T+1})
be a collection of bad error supports for the residue error.
There exists a family of bad fault paths
c
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{spacetime}}\subseteq P(J_{\mathsf{bulk}})
, which depends on
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}
, such that the following conditions hold.

•

Bounded residue error.

If
e
is
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{spacetime}}
-avoiding, then
y
is
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}
-avoiding.

•

No logical error on good input.
If additionally
e_{\text{in}}
is
\mathcal{B}^{(Z)}_{1/2}
-avoiding, then
\phi(\tilde{e}+\tilde{c})+y\in\rowspan(H_{X})
.

Additionally,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{spacetime}}
has weight enumerator

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{spacetime}};~x\right)}\leq|J|\cdot[O_{\Delta}(x)]^{d/40}+\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{B}^{(R)};~O_{\Delta}(x^{1/2})\right)}~.

(6.42)

Proof.

We construct
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{spacetime}}
out of three collection of bad fault paths.
Let
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{\mathsf{bulk}}]
denote the induced subgraph of
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{\mathsf{ext}}]
with vertices
J_{\mathsf{bulk}}
.
The first collection,
\mathcal{F}_{1}
, are error supports that have significant overlap with any connected cluster of size at least
d
in
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{\mathsf{bulk}}]
.
Recall that
\mathcal{CG}_{(m,t)}^{(H_{\mathsf{bulk}})}
are the
(m,t)
clustering sets in
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{\mathsf{bulk}}]
. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{1}=\boxplus_{m\geq d}\mathcal{CG}_{(m,\lceil m/10\rceil)}^{(H_{\mathsf{bulk}})}.

(6.43)

Now suppose
e
is a
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-avoiding fault, and let us consider the connected components of
\tilde{e}+\tilde{c}
.
There are three types of components:

Type 1.

Input components
that contain vertices in
J^{\mathrm{d}}_{1}
and do not contain vertices in
J^{\mathrm{s}}_{T}
.

Type 2.

Internal components
that do not contain vertices in
J^{\mathrm{d}}_{1}
and
J^{\mathrm{s}}_{T}
.

Type 3.

Residue components
that do not contain vertices in
J^{\mathrm{d}}_{1}
and contain vertices in
J^{\mathrm{s}}_{T}
.

In particular, there are no components that contain vertices in both
J^{\mathrm{d}}_{1}
and
J^{\mathrm{s}}_{T}
.
To see this, let
c
be the restriction of
\tilde{c}
onto
J_{\mathsf{bulk}}
. If
\tilde{e}+\tilde{c}
contains a connected component that traverses from
J^{\mathrm{d}}_{1}
to
J^{\mathrm{s}}_{T}
, then
e+c
must contain a connected component
C
that traverses from
J^{\mathrm{s}}_{1}
to
J^{\mathrm{s}}_{T}
, which means
|C|\geq d
.
Since
c
is the minimum weight correction to
e
, we must have
|e\cap C|\geq|C|/2\geq d/2
, which is ruled out by
\mathcal{F}_{1}
.

Observe that the same argument implies that all internal components must have size less than
d
.
By
Section

6.2
, these components are flattened to stabilizers. They induces no logical error and no residue syndrome. We can safely ignore them for the rest of this proof.

To bound the residue error
y
and therefore show the first condition, we can safely ignore input components and consider residue components.
Let
B
be a set in
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}
.
From
Section

6.2
, if
y
contains
B
, then there must be a cluster cover
R
of
B
in the extended graph
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{\mathsf{ext}}]
such that

1.

R\subseteq(e+c)\sqcup y
, and
|R|\geq 2|B|
;

2.

For every cluster
C
of
R
, we have
|(e+c)\cap C|\geq|C|/2
. Note that
C
must contain some vertex of
B
, which is in
J^{\mathrm{d}}_{T+1}
.

Taking the union over disjoint connected clusters
C
, we have

\displaystyle(e+c)\cap R\geq|R|/2.

(6.44)

Due to minimality of
c
, we have
|e\cap R|\geq|R|/4
.
For a set
B\subseteq J^{\mathrm{d}}_{T+1}
, recall that
\mathcal{CO}(r,B)
denote the collection of cluster covers of
B
of size at least
r
in
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{\mathsf{ext}}]
.
Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{2}=\boxplus_{B\subseteq J^{\mathrm{d}}_{T+1},B\in\mathcal{B}^{(R)}}\boxplus_{r\geq 2|B|}\boxplus_{R\in\mathcal{CO}(r,B)}\{T\subseteq R:|T|=|R|/4,\text{ and }T\subseteq J_{\mathsf{bulk}}\}.

(6.45)

We see that if
y
is not
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}
-avoiding, then
e
must contain a set from
\mathcal{F}_{2}
.
In other words, if
e
is
(\mathcal{F}_{1}\boxplus\mathcal{F}_{2})
-avoiding, then
y
is
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}
-avoiding, as desired.

Next, we want to rule out the case that
\phi(\tilde{e}+\tilde{c})+y\notin\rowspan(H_{X})
.
As discussed earlier, internal components do not induce logical errors when flattened.
For residue components, by
Section

6.2
,
there must exists a connected cluster
C\subset(e+c)\sqcup y
of size at least
d
in
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{\mathsf{ext}}]
(
C
needs to contain vertices in
J^{\mathrm{d}}_{T+1}
) such that
|(e+c)\cap C|\geq|C|/2
.
We have

\displaystyle|(e+c)\cap C|\geq|C|/2\Rightarrow|e\cap C|\geq|C|/4\geq d/4.

(6.46)

This is forbidden by the fact that
e
is
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-avoiding.
31
31

31

This is not directly implied by the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
, since it is defined over connected components in the bulk graph, while
C
is a connected component in the extended graph.
To prove our claim, we construct a connected cluster
C^{\prime}
in the bulk graph.
Let
z\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
be the restriction of
C
to
J^{\mathrm{d}}_{T+1}
.
Let
z^{\prime}
denote the same set (equivalently vector) of vertices as
z
, but supported over
J^{\mathrm{d}}_{T}
.
Let
C^{\prime}=C|_{J_{\mathsf{bulk}}}\cup z^{\prime}
(note that we used
\cup
instead of
\sqcup
here).
Then
C^{\prime}
is a connected component in the bulk graph of size at least
d
, and
|e\cap C^{\prime}|=|e\cap C|\geq d/4
.
This cannot happen since
e
is
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-avoiding.

Therefore residue components cannot induce logical errors when flattened.

Finally, we consider input components.
Let
\mathcal{T}
be the union of all input components.
Recall that in our decoding process (
Section

6.2
), we first computed
c
as a correction to
e
and then computed
c_{\text{in}}
as a correction to
e_{\text{in}}
.
Therefore
\mathcal{T}\cap J_{\mathsf{bulk}}=\mathcal{T}\cap(e+c)
, which has no spacetime syndrome under
H_{\mathsf{bulk}}
.
By
Section

6.2
, we have

\displaystyle H_{Z}\phi_{\mathsf{bulk}}(\mathcal{T}\cap(e+c))=(e+c)|_{J^{\mathrm{s}}_{1}}.

(6.47)

Let
f_{\text{in}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
be the minimum weight vector such that
H_{Z}f_{\text{in}}=(e+c)|_{J^{\mathrm{s}}_{1}}
, then
c_{\text{in}}
is the minimum weight vector such that
H_{Z}c_{\text{in}}=H_{Z}(e_{\text{in}}+f_{\text{in}})
.
We can now repeat our arguments above, as
f_{\text{in}}
is canonical to
y
.
Since
e
is
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-avoiding, we have that

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathsf{bulk}}(\mathcal{T}\cap(e+c))+f_{\text{in}}\in\rowspan(H_{X}).

(6.48)

Moreover, define

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{3}=\boxplus_{B\subseteq J^{\mathrm{d}}_{1},B\in\mathcal{B}^{(Z)}_{1/10}}\boxplus_{r\geq 2|B|}\boxplus_{R\in\mathcal{CO}(r,B)}\{T\subseteq R:|T|=|R|/4,\text{ and }T\subseteq J_{\mathsf{bulk}}\}.

(6.49)

If
e
is
\mathcal{F}_{3}
-avoiding, we know that

f_{\text{in}}
is
\mathcal{B}^{(Z)}_{1/10}
-avoiding.

Now suppose
e_{\text{in}}
is
\mathcal{B}^{(Z)}_{1/2}
-avoiding.
By
Section

6.1
,
e_{\text{in}}+f_{\text{in}}
is
\mathcal{B}^{(Z)}_{6/10}
-avoiding, which implies that
e_{\text{in}}+f_{\text{in}}+c_{\text{in}}\in\rowspan(H_{X})
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\phi(\mathcal{T})=e_{\text{in}}+c_{\text{in}}+\phi_{\mathsf{bulk}}(\mathcal{T}\cap(e+c))=(e_{\text{in}}+c_{\text{in}}+f_{\text{in}})+(f_{\text{in}}+\phi_{\mathsf{bulk}}(\mathcal{T}\cap(e+c)))\in\rowspan(H_{X}).

(6.50)

In other words, input components cannot induce logical errors.

Putting things together, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{spacetime}}=\mathcal{F}_{1}\boxplus\mathcal{F}_{2}\boxplus\mathcal{F}_{3},

(6.51)

and we have argued that if
e
is
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{spacetime}}
-avoiding, then both conditions hold.
It remains for us to bound the weight enumerator of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{spacetime}}
.
Let
\Delta_{H}
denote the maximum degree of
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{\mathsf{ext}}]
, we know that
\Delta_{H}=O(\Delta)
.
By the same calculation as in
Section

6.1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{1};~x\right)}\leq|J|\cdot[O_{\Delta}(x)]^{d/10}.

(6.52)

To bound
\mathcal{F}_{2}
, we consider bad error supports in
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}
.
Invoking
Footnote

29
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{2};~x\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{B\in\mathcal{B}^{(R)}}\sum_{r\geq 2|B|}\sum_{R\in\mathcal{CO}(r,B)}2^{|R|}x^{|R|/4}

(6.53)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{B\in\mathcal{B}^{(R)}}\sum_{r\geq 2|B|}(\Delta_{H}e)^{r}2^{r}x^{r/4}

(6.54)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{B\in\mathcal{B}^{(R)}}[O_{\Delta}(x)]^{|B|/2}

(6.55)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{B}^{(R)};~O_{\Delta}(x^{1/2})\right)}~.

(6.56)

To bound
\mathcal{F}_{3}
, we need to consider
\mathcal{B}^{(Z)}_{1/10}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{3};~x\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{B\in\mathcal{B}^{(Z)}_{1/10}}\sum_{r\geq 2|B|}\sum_{R\in\mathcal{CO}(r,B)}2^{r}x^{r/4}

(6.57)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{B\in\mathcal{B}^{(Z)}_{1/10}}\sum_{r\geq 2|B|}(\Delta_{H}e)^{r}2^{r}x^{r/4}

(6.58)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{B}^{(Z)}_{1/10};~O_{\Delta}(x^{1/2})\right)}

(6.59)

\displaystyle\leq|J|\cdot[O_{\Delta}(x)]^{d/40}.

(6.60)

Therefore, putting everything together we see that

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{spacetime}};~x\right)}

\displaystyle\leq|J|\cdot[O_{\Delta}(x)]^{d/40}+\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{B}^{(R)};~O_{\Delta}(x^{1/2})\right)}.\qed

(6.61)

6.3
Error correction gadget

{theorem}

[Error correction gadget
[
Den+02
,
Got14
]
]
We can construct a gadget
\mathsf{Gad}_{\mathsf{EC}}=(V,E,\mathsf{conn},\mathsf{type},\mathsf{gate})

of depth
O(d)
for the computational code with the following guarantee.

1.

For input code type
\mathsf{CompCode}_{1/2}=(U,\mathcal{B}_{1/2})
and any output code type
(U,\mathcal{B}^{(R)})
, let
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}_{1},\mathcal{B}^{(R)}_{2}
be two collections
32
32

32

Here we seperate the output error
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}
into two collections to account for two sources of residue errors: one from the decoder ouput as in
Section

6.2
, and another from the last round of syndrome extraction circuit.

such that
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}
is separable into
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}_{1},\mathcal{B}^{(R)}_{2}
as defined in
Section

6.1
.
There is a family of bad fault paths
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC}}
such that
\mathsf{Gad}_{\mathsf{EC}}
is a fault-tolerant gadget for identity with respect to the input/output code types and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC}}
.

2.

This family of bad fault paths
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC}}
has weight enumerator

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC}};~x\right)}

\displaystyle\leq d\cdot n\left(O_{\Delta}(x)\right)^{\frac{d}{80\Delta}}+\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{B}^{(R)}_{1};~O_{\Delta}(x^{\frac{1}{4\Delta}})\right)}+\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{B}^{(R)}_{2};~O_{\Delta}(x^{\frac{1}{2\Delta}})\right)}~.

(6.62)

Construction.
Syndrome extraction circuit.

First, we construct a constant-depth syndrome extraction circuit for the code in the standard way.
A
\Delta
-qLDPC code defined by the check matrices
(H_{X},H_{Z})
has a depth
\Delta+2
syndrome extraction circuit each for the
X
syndrome and the
Z
syndrome.
Let us consider the case of measuring the
Z
syndrome (
X
type operators associated with
H_{X}
).
The case of the
X
syndrome is identical with
\mathsf{CNOT}
replaced by
\mathsf{CZ}
and
H_{X}
replaced by
H_{Z}
.

First, compute an edge coloring of the Tanner graph of
H_{Z}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r\times n}
:
Define the bipartite graph
G=([r]\sqcup[n],E)
where two vertices
(i,j)\in[r]\times[n]
are adjacent if
H[i,j]=1
.

G
has maximum degree
\Delta
by the
\Delta
-qLDPC property.
The edge coloring of
G
is a map
\phi\colon E\mapsto[\Delta]
such that no vertex has two edges of the same color incident to it.
Equivalently, for each color
c\in[\Delta]
, the subset of edges
\phi^{-1}(m)
is a matching in
G
.

G
is bipartite so such a map exists and is efficiently computable
[
Sch+03
]
.

A single round of syndrome extraction is as follows:

1.

Prepare
r
ancilla qubits in
\ket{+}
.

2.

For each color
c\in[\Delta]
, perform the gate layer:

•

For each edge
(i,j)\in\phi^{-1}(c)
, apply
\mathsf{CNOT}(i,j)
(controlled on the ancilla qubit
i
)

3.

Measure all
r
ancilla qubits in the
X
basis. The
i
-th measurement outcome is the eigenvalue of the
X
operator associated with the
i
-th row of the check matrix
H_{X}
.

Let us refer to a single round of syndrome
Z
and
X
-type syndrome extraction as the circuit
M
.
The full error correction gadget alternates layers of
Z
and
X
-type syndrome extraction rounds
T
(to be picked later) rounds.

That is, the gadget applies
M
for
T
times where each application of
M
should be interpreted as producing an
Z
and
X
measurement vector
m_{1},\dots,m_{T}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r_{X}+r_{Z}}
.

\blacktriangleleft

Proof.

From these measurments, we will compute a correction and apply it.

Decoding.

Let
r_{X}
be the number of rows of
H_{X}
and
r_{Z}
be the number of rows of
H_{Z}
.
The syndrome extraction circuit produces a pair of measurements
m_{X}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r_{X}\times T}
and
m_{Z}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r_{Z}\times T}
.
We take pairwise differences to obtain syndromes
s_{X}
and
s_{Z}
.

\displaystyle s_{X/Z}[I_{1}]

\displaystyle=m_{X/Z}[I_{1}]

(6.63)

\displaystyle t\in[T-1],\quad s_{X/Z}[I_{t+1}]

\displaystyle=m_{X/Z}[I_{t}]+m_{X/Z}[I_{t+1}]

(6.64)

In what follows, to reduce notation, we will not distinguish between diagonal Pauli superoperators and Pauli operators.
Additionally, for a bit string
x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{m}
, we will also use
x
to denote the state
\outerproduct{x}{x}
when clear from context.
For now, suppose that the fault
\mathbf{f}
is a diagonal Pauli fault and the input state
\rho_{\mathrm{0}}
is
\mathcal{B}_{c}
-deviated from a state
\mathsf{enc}(\sigma)
by a diagonal Pauli superoperator
E_{0}

\displaystyle\mathsf{map}[M^{\otimes t}[\mathbf{f}]](\rho_{0})=\rho_{t}\otimes m_{X}[I_{1}]\otimes m_{Z}[I_{1}]\otimes\dots\otimes m_{X}[I_{t}]\otimes m_{Z}[I_{t}]

(6.65)

be the state (including measurement outputs) after the
t
-th round of
Z
and
X
syndrome extraction, and
E_{t}
be the diagonal Pauli superoperator supported on the code block that takes
\rho_{t-1}
to
\rho_{t}
,
E_{t}(\rho_{t-1})=\rho_{t}
.
Let us write
\xi
for the syndrome map
\mathcal{P}^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r_{X}+r_{Z}}
.
For
t\in[T]
, define
\tilde{m}_{t}=m_{t}-\xi(E_{t-1}\dots E_{0})
to be the difference between the measured syndrome and syndrome of the state input to the
t
-th application of
M
, and decompose
E_{t}=E_{t}^{(X)}E_{t}^{(Z)}
into the product of Pauli
X
and Pauli
Z
.
Using that, in the absence of any further faults, the measurement output is the syndrome of the state and that
\xi
is a group homomorphism,
\xi(AB)=\xi(A)+\xi(B)
, we can see that the syndrome differences (
Eq.

6.63
give the syndrome of any new errors applied up to measurement errors.

\displaystyle s_{X/Z}[I_{1}]

\displaystyle=\xi(E_{0}^{(Z/X)})+\tilde{m}_{X/Z}[I_{1}]

(6.66)

\displaystyle t\in[T-1],\quad s_{X/Z}[I_{t+1}]

\displaystyle=\xi(E_{t-1}^{(Z/X)})+\tilde{m}_{X/Z}[I_{t}]+\tilde{m}_{X/Z}[I_{t+1}]

(6.67)

For convenience, we will decode these syndromes separately.
We focus on decoding
X
errors with syndrome
s_{Z}
.
The case of
Z
errors is identical.

We now decode the syndrome
s_{Z}
with respect to the spacetime code
H
of
H_{Z}
, as specified by the decoding procedure in
Section

6.2
, to produce a correction
c_{X}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{(Tn+Tr_{z})}
.
We apply an
X
correction to the
i
-th qubit when the
i
-th bit of the flattening
\phi
of the correction
c_{X}
is
1
,
C_{X}=X^{\phi(c_{X})}
.

The overall gadget
\mathsf{Gad}_{\mathsf{EC}}
is then given in figure
Fig.

7
. (Runtime of the decoder ignored.)

Figure 7:
Error correction gadget
\mathsf{Gad}_{\mathsf{EC}}
, the circuit
M
is repeated
T
times.
