Fault analysis.

We would now like to analyze the residual error.
We will set
T=d
.
First note that there is an error
e\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{(Tn+Tr_{z})}
of the spacetime code corresponding to the error that in the circuit that yields the measured syndrome differences.

\displaystyle t\in[T],\quad E^{(X)}_{t-1}=X^{e[J_{t}^{\mathrm{d}}]}

(6.68)

\displaystyle t\in[T],\quad\tilde{m}_{Z}[I_{t}]=e[J_{t}^{\mathrm{s}}]

(6.69)

With the exception of the final round error, the flattening also matches the residual error:
X^{\phi(e)}=E^{(X)}_{T-1}\dots E_{0}^{(X)}
.
We now utilize the obvious bijection between the supports of
E^{(X)}_{T-1},\dots,E^{(X)}_{0}
,
\tilde{m}_{Z}
, and bits of the spacetime code
[Tn+Tr_{z}]
.
For a bit
i\in[Tn+Tr_{z}]
, let
\chi_{i}\subseteq V
denote the set of locations of
\mathsf{Gad}_{\mathsf{EC}}
for which a fault may flip bit
i
.
A fault supported on a single location may flip at most
s=2\Delta
bits of the spacetime code: A two-qubit gate failure affects a data and ancilla qubit. and each of these has a gate shared with
\Delta-1
other qubits.
33
33

33

These are the well known “hook errors.” A more careful counting would should that the majority of the spread of the error does not intersection any of the sets of interest in so many places.

Likewise, a single bit of the spacetime code may be flipped by a fault at one of at most
\Delta(2\Delta+4)
locations.

Define a bipartite graph
G=(V\sqcup[Tn+Tr],E)
between the locations of the gadget and the spacetime code bits.
Bit
i
is connected to spacetime location
v
if
v\in\chi_{i}
.

G
has left degree at most
2\Delta
and right degree at most
\Delta(2\Delta+4)
.
We can now define a family of bad fault in the gadget corresponding to the bad spacetime code errors.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{spacetime}}^{(X)}
be the family of bad error supports in the statement of
Section

6.2
, with the guarantee that the residue error is
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}_{1}
-avoiding.
For a subset of vertices
S\subseteq[Tn+Tr]
, let
N(S)
denote the neighborhood of
S
in
G
.
If a location is capable of flipping a particular spacetime code bit then the two must be adjacent in
G
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC},1}^{(X)}=\boxplus_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{spacetime}}^{(X)}}\{f^{\prime}\subseteq N(f)\mid\forall u\in f,\hskip 4.30554pt|N(u)\cap f^{\prime}|>0\}

(6.70)

There are at most
2^{|f|\Delta(2\Delta+4)}
subsets of
N(f)
and each subset must have weight at least
|f|/(2\Delta)
.
Thus for
x\in[0,1]
, we can upper bound the weight enumerator as

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC},1}^{(X)};~x\right)}\leq\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{spacetime}}^{(X)};~2^{\Delta(2\Delta+4)}x^{\frac{1}{2\Delta}}\right)}

(6.71)

We repeat this construction with
X
and
Z
interchanged to obtain
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC},1}^{(Z)}
.

We also need to bound the error caused by the last application of
M
that propagates to
E_{T}
.
We can perform an identical argument with
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{spacetime}}^{(X)}
replaced by
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}_{2}
and bits of the spacetime code replaced by
E_{T}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC},2};~x\right)}\leq\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{B}^{(R)}_{2};~2^{\Delta(2\Delta+4)}x^{\frac{1}{2\Delta}}\right)}

(6.72)

Our bad fault paths are

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC}}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC},1}^{(X)}\boxplus\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC},1}^{(Z)}\boxplus\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC},2}~.

(6.73)

Now, let
\mathbf{f}
be a
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC}}
-avoiding diagonal Pauli fault.
In the execution of
M[\mathbf{f}]
on a state
\rho_{0}
, the spacetime code error
e
(
Eq.

6.68
) is
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC},1}^{(X)}
-avoiding in the
Z
-type syndrome extraction and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC},1}^{(Z)}
-avoiding in the
X
-type syndrome extraction.
In the case where
E_{0}
is
\mathcal{B}_{1/2}
-avoiding, it follows by
Section

6.2
that
C_{X}C_{Z}E_{T-1}\dots E_{0}
is
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}_{1}
-avoiding.
Otherwise, there exists a logical operator of the code
L
such that
LC_{X}C_{Z}E_{T-1}\dots E_{0}
is
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}_{1}
-avoiding (friendly).

The final round of errors
E_{T}
has
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}_{2}
-avoiding support,
by the separability of
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}
as
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}_{1}
and
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}_{2}
, the output is
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}
-deviated from the codespace.
In the case that the input is
\mathcal{B}_{1/2}
-deviated from the encoding of a logical state
\mathsf{enc}(\sigma)
, then the output is
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}
-deviated from
\mathsf{enc}(\sigma)
.

We now drop the assumption that the faults are diagonal: For a general input error and faults (non-diagonal Pauli)
Section

4.4
can be applied at each step, so that the errors at each step is either equivalent to a diagonal Pauli error (the left and right sides are stabilizer equivalent) up to some part in the lightcone of the fault in that round or the state is annihilated by the measurement projectors (so the deviation property trivially holds with coefficient
0
).

We now plug in the bounds on the weight enumerators to obtain (recall
T
was picked to be
d
so there are at most
\propto nd
locations in the spacetime graph.)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC}};~x\right)}

\displaystyle\leq d\cdot n[O_{\Delta}(x)]^{\frac{d}{80\Delta}}+\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{B}^{(R)}_{1};~O_{\Delta}(x^{\frac{1}{4\Delta}})\right)}+\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{B}^{(R)}_{2};~O_{\Delta}(x^{\frac{1}{2\Delta}})\right)}~.

(6.74)

Following the definition of a friendly, fault-tolerant gadget
Section

4.1
, we see that this entire error correction gadget implements the filter superoperator
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{f}}
and an identity logical gate,
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathbf{f}}=\mathsf{I}
.
∎

{remark}

[Friendliness]
Through
Section

6.2
, we have argued that the error correction gadget is friendly. I.e., when the input error is bad, the residue error of the output state is still bounded, although the logical state is erroroneous.
By a closer inspection of the gadget construction and decoding, we note that the EC gadget is
friendly in both the
X
and
Z
bases
.
More precisely, suppose the input error is a diagonal Pauli error
P_{X}P_{Z}
.
If
P_{X}
has
\mathcal{B}_{1/5}
-avoiding support while
P_{Z}
is arbitrarily supported, the logical error
L
will be a
Z
error.
Similarly, if
P_{Z}
has
\mathcal{B}_{1/5}
-avoiding support while
P_{X}
is arbitrarily supported, the logical error
L
will be a
X
error.

In later constructions of fault-tolerance schemes, we will often construct a logical gate gadget by first applying this error correction gadget, followed by the corresponding logical operation.
The constructed gadgets are therefore friendly and the error support on the input state to the logical operation can be easily bounded.

{remark}

[Single-shot decoding]
The error correction gadget constructed above repeatedly measure the sydnrome information for
O(d)
rounds, which protects the encoded information from measurement errors.
For most codes this repetition is necessary to control the residue error distribution.
There are families of qLDPC codes which can be single-shot decoded
[
Bom15
,
KV22
,
BKV24
,
FGL20
,
Gu+24
,
NP24
]
.
More precisely, these codes have efficient decoders which only requires
O(1)
rounds of syndrome information to compute corrections with logical error rates
e^{-O(d)}
and controlled residue errors.
Using these codes as computation code could improves the depth overhead of fault-tolerant gadgets.
It is worth noting, however, that an error correction gadget constructed from a single-shot decoder may not be friendly.
For instance, the decoder of
[
Gu+24
]
, which is a modified version of the decoder proposed by
[
LZ23
]
, assumes that the input qubit and measurement error weights are both upper bounded by
cn
for some constant
c
(the decoded code is asymptotically good).
For the bad input state where this assumption is not satisfied, the decoder makes no guarantee on the output state and residue error.
Conceptually this can be seen as a
logical leakage error
, where not only is the logical information corrupted, the logical qubit itself is also lost and requires reinitialization (for instance, by a friendly gadget).

{remark}

[Code switching]
The error correction gadget we constructed measures the same set of stabilizers repeatedly and implements a logical identity.
Alternatively, by measuring different groups of stabilizers corresponding to different codes, we can fault-tolerantly switch from one code (and code type) to another
[
BMD09
]
.
Such
code-switching
protocols are highly versatile.
One motivating example is to switch between a two-dimensional topological code which admits transversal Clifford gates
[
CS96
,
Ste96
,
BMD06
]
and a three-dimensional topological code which admits transversal non-Clifford gates
[
Bom15
,
KB15
,
PR13
,
KYP15
]
, see
[
Bom16
]
.
This enables one to utilize two computational codes and implement a universal gate set transversally.

Another well-studied type of code-switching is
code surgery
, which implements multi-qubit Pauli measurement on logical qubits.
Such multi-qubit Pauli measurement, when supplied with magic states, can be used to implement universal quantum computation in the framework of Pauli-based compuation (PBC)
[
BSS16
]
.
The prototypical example of code surgery is lattice surgery
[
Hor+12
,
Fow+12
]
on surface codes, which can be applied to design fault-tolerant surface code architectures with two-dimensional nearnest neighbor connectivity
[
FG18
,
Lit19
]
.
These methods are later generalized to other qLDPC codes
[
Has17
,
Has21
,
Coh+22
]
under the name
qLDPC surgery
.
While the first proposals of qLDPC surgery
[
Coh+22
]
incurs a heavy space overhead, recent works
[
Cow24
,
Cro+24
,
WY24
,
Ide+24
,
SJOY24
,
ZL24
]
presented improved proposals with significantly lower theoretical and practical overheads.
These methods are subsequently improved and applied in
[
Cow+25
,
He+25
,
Yod+25
]
.
In particular, the extractor architectures proposed in
[
He+25
]
enables fault-tolerant PBC on arbitrary qLDPC codes with low-overhead and fixed connectivity.
It would be interesting to prove the fault-tolerance of these surgery gadgets in our weight enumerator formalism.

The aforementioned examples of code-switching can all be described using the langauge of subsystem codes and gauge-fixing
[
Vui+19
]
.
In contrast, the dynamical codes proposed in
[
HH21
]
demonstrated that logical information can be stored and operated on in a continuous sequence of code-switching
[
Dav+24
]
.
It would be interesting to describe the fault-tolerance properties of these dynamically generated logical qubits in terms of weight enumerators as well.

6.4
Constant depth gadgets

Gate gadgets for transversal gates are a short corollary.

{corollary}
[Transversal gate gadget]
Let
\mathcal{G}
be a gate on either one or two computational code blocks that can be implemented transversally.
Then, there exists a gadget for
\mathcal{G}
with input and output code types
\mathsf{CompCode}_{1/5}
and a family of bad fault paths
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{G}}
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{G}};~x\right)}\leq 2\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC}};~x\right)}+\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{B}_{1/10};~x\right)}\leq d\cdot n\cdot[O_{\Delta}(x)]^{\frac{d}{80\Delta}}.

(6.75)

Proof.

We consider the more complicated case of two code blocks.

We execute the error correction gadget
Section

6.3
on the two inputs block,
setting
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}=\mathcal{B}_{1/5}
separable into two copies of
\mathcal{B}_{1/10}
.
From
Section

6.1
, we know

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{B}_{1/10};~x\right)}

\displaystyle\leq n\cdot[O_{\Delta}(x)]^{\frac{d}{20}}.

(6.76)

Combined with
Eq.

6.62
, we have an upper bound on the weight enumerator of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC}}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC}};~x\right)}\leq d\cdot n\cdot[O_{\Delta}(x)]^{\frac{d}{80\Delta}}.

(6.77)

We pick
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{G}}
to be the sum of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC}}
on the two error correction gadgets and
\mathcal{B}_{1/10}
on the transversal gates.
The upper bound in our claim follows.

Assume the fault is
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{G}}
-avoiding. Then, if the input state
\rho
is
\mathcal{B}_{1/2}\boxplus\mathcal{B}_{1/2}
-deviated from a logical state
(\mathsf{enc}\otimes\mathsf{enc})(\sigma)
, then the output of the error correction gadgets is
\mathcal{B}_{1/5}\boxplus\mathcal{B}_{1/5}
-deviated from
(\mathsf{enc}\otimes\mathsf{enc})(\sigma)
.
The transversal gate gadget can at most create a
\mathcal{B}_{1/10}
-avoiding error and spread a
\mathcal{B}_{1/5}
-avoiding error to each block.
It follows by
Section

6.1
that the output state is
\mathcal{B}_{1/2}\boxplus\mathcal{B}_{1/2}
-deviated from
(\mathsf{enc}\otimes\mathsf{enc})\circ\mathcal{G}(\sigma)
.

For an arbitrary input state, the output of the error correction gadgets is still
\mathcal{B}_{1/5}\boxplus\mathcal{B}_{1/5}
-deviated from the code space.
∎

Note that we precede all transversal gates with a
O(d)
-round error correction gadget, which incurs a notable time (equivalently depth) overhead in theoretical and practical fault-tolerance.
Alternatively, we could interleave transversal gates with syndrome extraction rounds to amortize the time overhead of a full error correction gadgets across multiple logical operations.
This approach forms the backbone of
transversal algorithmic fault-tolerance
as proposed and studied in
[
Zho+24
]
.
It is an interesting future direction to formulate this approach in terms of gadgets and weight enumerators.

Beyond transversal gates, we further consider constant depth gates supported on one computational code block, and show the standard claim that constant depth gates are fault tolerant via a lightcone argument.

{lemma}

[Constant depth gate gadget]
Let
\mathcal{G}
be a gate on one computational code block that can be implemented by a circuit
C_{\mathcal{G}}
of depth
0pt
.
Set
c=\frac{1}{5(T+1)2^{T+1}}
.
Then there exists a gadget for
\mathcal{G}
with input and output code type
\mathsf{CompCode}_{1/5}
and a family of bad fault paths
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{G}}
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{G}};~x\right)}\leq dn\cdot[O_{\Delta}(x)]^{\frac{d}{80\Delta}}+n\cdot[O_{\Delta,T}(x)]^{\frac{cd}{16\Delta}}+Tn\cdot[O_{\Delta,T}(x)]^{\frac{cd}{2}}.

(6.78)

Proof.

We will first apply the error correction gadget
Section

6.3
on the code block, with
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}
to be specified later.
The residue error being
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}
-avoiding and the fault path on
C_{\mathcal{G}}
being
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{G}}
-avoiding will guarantee that the final output error is
\mathcal{B}_{1/5}
-avoiding.

We use a lightcone argument to define
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{G}}
.
Since
C_{\mathcal{G}}=(V,E,\mathsf{conn},\mathsf{type},\mathsf{gate})
acts on the code qubits labelled by
[n]
and has depth
T
, we partition the edges in
C_{\mathcal{G}}
by depth as
E=\cup_{t=1}^{T+1}E_{t}
, where
E_{1},E_{T+1}
are the input and output qubits respectively and each
E_{t}
is a copy of
[n]
.
We denote the qubits in
E_{t}
as
e_{i}^{t}
, for
i\in[n]
, with the implicit identification that
\{e_{i}^{t}\}_{t=1}^{T+1}
denote the same physical qubit at different time.
For a qubit
e_{i}^{t}\in E_{t}
, the
forward lightcone
of
e_{i}^{t}
, denoted
\mathcal{LC}(e_{i}^{t})
, are all qubits
e^{\prime}\in E_{T+1}
such that there is a directed path from
e
to
e^{\prime}
in the directed network of
C_{\mathcal{G}}
.
Observe that for a qubit
e_{i}
and two time steps
t_{1}<t_{2}
, we have

\mathcal{LC}(e_{i}^{t_{1}})\supseteq\mathcal{LC}(e_{i}^{t_{1}})
.
Note further that for all
i\in[n]
,
|\mathcal{LC}(e_{i}^{1})|\leq 2^{T}
since we assumed that every gate in
C_{\mathcal{G}}
acts on at most two qubits.

To account for error propagation through the circuit
C_{\mathcal{G}}
, we define the
lightcone adjacency graph
, which we denote
G_{\mathcal{LC}}
.

G_{\mathcal{LC}}
has vertices corresponding to qubits
[n]
of the computational code.
Given the stabilizer check matrices
H_{X},H_{Z}
, we add an edge between any pair of two qubits which are acted on by at least one common stabilizer.
In other words,
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{X}],G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{Z}]
are both subgraphs of
G_{\mathcal{LC}}
.
Further, for every qubit
e_{i}
, for every qubit
e_{j}
such that
e_{j}^{T+1}\in\mathcal{LC}(e_{i}^{1})
, we add an edge between
e_{i},e_{j}
.
This completes our definition of
G_{\mathcal{LC}}
.
The maximum degree of
G_{\mathcal{LC}}
, denoted
\Delta_{\mathcal{LC}}
, satisfies
\Delta_{\mathcal{LC}}=O_{\Delta,T}(1)
.

We now bound the output error support
B^{\text{out}}\subseteq E_{T+1}
using the support of the input error
B_{1}\subseteq E_{1}
and the fault path
F\subseteq V
.
Recall that the vertices of
C_{\mathcal{G}}
is partitioned into depth,
V=\cup_{t=1}^{T}V_{t}
.
We similarly partition the fault path
F=\cup_{t=1}^{T}F_{t}
, where
F_{t}=F\cap V_{t}
.
For each
F_{t}
, let
B_{t+1}\subseteq E_{t+1}
denote the output qubits of vertices in
F_{t}
, and note that
|B_{t+1}|\leq 2|F_{t}|
.
We define the lightcone of a set of qubits simply as the union of lightcones of individual qubits.

\displaystyle\mathcal{LC}(B):=\cup_{e\in B}\mathcal{LC}(e).

(6.79)

By the definition of lightcone, we note that faults on a set of qubits
B\subseteq E
can propagate to only qubits in
\mathcal{LC}(B)\subseteq E_{T+1}
.
This implies that

\displaystyle B^{\text{out}}\subseteq\mathcal{LC}\left(\bigcup_{t=1}^{T+1}B_{t}\right)=\bigcup_{t=1}^{T+1}\mathcal{LC}(B_{t}).

(6.80)

It suffices for us to bound the error supports of
\mathcal{LC}(B)
.
We define bad error supports
\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{LC}}_{c}\subseteq P([n])
according to the graph
G_{\mathcal{LC}}
.
Let
\mathcal{CG}_{m,m^{\prime}}
denote the
(m,m^{\prime})
-clustering sets in
G_{\mathcal{LC}}
.
Similar to
Section

6.1
, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{LC}}_{c}=\boxplus_{m=d}^{n}\mathcal{CG}_{m,\lceil cm/2\rceil}.

(6.81)

From the definition of
G_{\mathcal{LC}}
, we have the following claim.

{claim}

For all
t\leq T+1
, if
B\subseteq E_{t}
is
\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{LC}}_{c/2^{T+1}}
-avoiding, then
\mathcal{LC}(B)\subseteq E_{T+1}
is
\mathcal{B}_{c}
-avoiding.

Proof of claim.
Suppose for the sake of contradiction
\mathcal{LC}(B)
contains a set
S\subseteq E_{T+1}\cong[n]
such that
S
is a subset of a connected set
\bar{S}
of size
m\geq d
and
|S|\geq\lceil cm/2\rceil
.
Since
\bar{S}
is a connected set in
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{X}]
or
G_{\mathsf{adj}}[H_{X}]
, which are both subgraphs of
G_{\mathcal{LC}}
,
\bar{S}
must be connected in
G_{\mathcal{LC}}
as well.
Let
N(\bar{S})
denote the neighborhood of
\bar{S}
in
G_{\mathcal{LC}}
.
Then

\displaystyle|B\cap(N(\bar{S})\cup\bar{S})|\geq\frac{|S|}{2^{T}}\geq\frac{c|\bar{S}|}{2^{T+1}},

(6.82)

since every lightcone has size bounded by
2^{T}
.
For convenience let
R:=B\cap(N(\bar{S})\cup S)
.
Then
R\cup\bar{S}
is a connected set of vertices in
G_{\mathcal{LC}}
of size at least
d
, and

\displaystyle\frac{c|R\cup\bar{S}|}{2^{T+2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c|\bar{S}|}{2^{T+2}}+\frac{c|R|}{2^{T+2}}\leq|R|.

(6.83)

This contradicts our assumption that
B
is
\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{LC}}_{c/2^{T+1}}
-avoiding, which proves our claim.

\blacksquare

Set
c=\frac{1}{5(T+1)2^{T+1}}
.
Following
Section

6.1
, we see that if
B_{t}
is
\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{LC}}_{c}
-avoiding for all
t\leq T+1
, then
B^{\text{out}}
is
\mathcal{B}_{1/5}
-avoiding.
Therefore, we set
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}=\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{LC}}_{c}
, which is separable as two copies of
\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{LC}}_{c/2}
.
The EC gadget,
Section

6.3
, gives us
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC}}
with bounded weight enumerator.

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC}};~x\right)}

\displaystyle\leq dn\cdot[O_{\Delta}(x)]^{\frac{d}{80\Delta}}+2\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{LC}}_{c/2};~O_{\Delta}(x^{\frac{1}{4\Delta}})\right)}

(6.84)

\displaystyle\leq dn\cdot[O_{\Delta}(x)]^{\frac{d}{80\Delta}}+n\cdot[O_{\Delta,T}(x)]^{\frac{cd}{16\Delta}}.

(6.85)

We further define the bad fault paths on the circuit
C_{\mathcal{G}}
,
\mathcal{F}_{C}
, as follows.
For every
t\leq T
, for
e\in E_{t+1}
, let
s(e)\subseteq V_{t}
denote the locations at time
t
that has
e
as an output qubit.
We define

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{C,t}=\boxplus_{B\in\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{LC}}_{c}}\{F_{t}\subseteq V_{t}\mid\forall e\in B,|s(e)\cap F_{t}|>0\}.

(6.86)

There are at most
2|B|
locations in
V_{t}
with output qubits overlapping with
B
, so there are at most
2^{2|B|}
such sets
F_{t}
. Each set must have weight at least
|B|/2
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{C,t};~x\right)}\leq\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{LC}}_{c};~4x^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)}.

(6.87)

We define
\mathcal{F}_{C}
to be the product of
\mathcal{F}_{C,t}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{C}

\displaystyle=\boxplus_{t=1}^{T}\mathcal{F}_{C,t}

(6.88)

Invoking
Section

6.1
,
the weight enumerator can be bounded as

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{C};~x\right)}

\displaystyle\leq T\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{LC}}_{c};~4x^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)}

(6.89)

\displaystyle\leq Tn\cdot[O_{\Delta,T}(x)]^{\frac{cd}{2}}.

(6.90)

Define
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{G}}=\mathcal{F}_{C}\boxplus\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC}}
, and the bound on its weight enumerator follows.
∎

{lemma}

[CSS Code Initialization]
Let
\mathsf{INIT}_{\mathsf{Z}}
be the gate that initializes
k
qubits to the
\ket{0}
state. There exists a gadget for
\mathsf{INIT}_{\mathsf{Z}}
with trivial input code type and output code type
\mathsf{CompCode}_{1/5}
and a family of bad fault paths
\mathcal{F}
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F};~x\right)}\leq d\cdot n\cdot[O_{\Delta}(x)]^{\frac{d}{80\Delta}}.

(6.91)

Proof.

We use
\mathsf{INIT}_{\mathsf{Z}}^{\otimes n}
to initialize
n
qubits to the
\ket{0}
state, and then apply the error correction gadget
Section

6.3
with
\mathcal{B}^{(R)}=\mathcal{B}_{1/5}
separable into two copies of
\mathcal{B}_{1/10}
.
Define the bad fault paths
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{INIT}}
on the locations of the transversal initialization to be
\mathcal{B}_{1/10}

We define the overall bad fault paths
\mathcal{F}
to be
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{INIT}}\boxplus\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{EC}}
.
The upper bound on the weight enumerator then follows from the same analysis as in
Section

6.4
.

It remains for us to show that this gadget behaves correctly. We make use of the following fact.

{fact}

For a CSS code with
\mathsf{X}
parity check matrix
H_{X}
, let
m
be the dimension of
\rowspan(H_{X})
, i.e.,
|\rowspan(H_{X})|=2^{m}
.
Let
\ket{0_L}^{\otimes k}
denote the logical
\ket{0^k}
state of the code.
It holds that

\displaystyle\ket{0}^{\otimes n}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{m/2}}{2^{n}}\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}\mathsf{Z}^{v}\ket{0_L}^{\otimes k}.

(6.92)

As a corollary, it holds that

\displaystyle\outerproduct{0}{0}^{\otimes n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{2n-m}}\sum_{u,v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}\mathsf{Z}^{u}\mathsf{enc}(\outerproduct{0}{0}^{\otimes k})\mathsf{Z}^{v}.

(6.93)

Proof of claim.
Recall that for a CSS code,

\displaystyle\ket{0_L}^{\otimes k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{m/2}}\sum_{r\in\rowspan(H_{X})}\ket{r}.

(6.94)

We can thereby compute

\displaystyle\frac{2^{m/2}}{2^{n}}\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}\mathsf{Z}^{v}\ket{0_L}^{\otimes k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\sum_{r\in\rowspan(H_{X})}\left(\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}\mathsf{Z}^{v}\ket{r}\right)

(6.95)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\sum_{r\in\rowspan(H_{X})}\left(\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}(-1)^{v\cdot r}\ket{r}\right)

(6.96)

For
r\neq 0^{n}
, the sum
\sum_{v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}(-1)^{v\cdot r}\ket{r}
evaluates to 0. The only term that remains is
\ket{0}^{\otimes n}
, which proves this claim.

\blacksquare

Let
\mathbf{f}
be a
\mathcal{F}
-avoiding Pauli fault, which can be divided into
\mathbf{f}_{1}
supported on locations of
\mathsf{INIT}_{\mathsf{Z}}^{\otimes n}
and
\mathbf{f}_{2}
supported on locations of the error correction gadget.
Suppose the faulty circuit
\mathsf{INIT}_{\mathsf{Z}}^{\otimes n}[\mathbf{f}_{1}]
introduces an error
\mathcal{E}
, whose support is
\mathcal{B}_{1/5}
-avoiding, onto the state
\outerproduct{0}{0}^{\otimes n}
.
In the error correction gadget, we repeatedly measure the stabilizers of the CSS code, which enables us to invoke a variant of the decoherence of errors lemma (
Section

4.4
) on the input state. Specifically, consider the Kraus decomposition of
\mathcal{E}
in terms of Pauli operators
\{K_{\mu}\}_{\mu},\{K_{\nu}^{\prime}\}_{\nu}
and complex coefficients
\{\alpha_{\mu,\nu}\}_{\mu,\nu}
.
The measurement channel can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\text{syndrome }s}\outerproduct{s}{s}\otimes\Pi_{s}\rho\Pi_{s},

(6.97)

where
\Pi_{s}
is the projector onto the syndrome eigenspace corresponding to
s
.

\displaystyle\Pi_{s}\mathcal{E}(\outerproduct{0}{0}^{\otimes n})\Pi_{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{2n-m}}\sum_{\mu,\nu}\alpha_{\mu,\nu}\Pi_{s}K_{\mu}\left(\sum_{u,v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}}\mathsf{Z}^{u}\mathsf{enc}(\outerproduct{0}{0}^{\otimes k})\mathsf{Z}^{v}\right)K_{\nu}^{\prime}\Pi_{s}

(6.98)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{2n-m}}\sum_{\mu,\nu,u,v}\alpha_{\mu,\nu}\Pi_{s}K_{\mu}\mathsf{Z}^{u}\mathsf{enc}(\outerproduct{0}{0}^{\otimes k})\mathsf{Z}^{v}K_{\nu}^{\prime}\Pi_{s}.

(6.99)

As in the proof of
Section

4.4
, only terms where both
K_{\mu}\mathsf{Z}^{u}
and
K_{\nu}^{\prime}\mathsf{Z}^{v}
has the same syndrome
s
passes the projectors while the other terms are annihilated.
Moreover, since we have a CSS code, the
X
and
Z
errors have independent syndromes.
All
X
errors must come from the operators
K_{\mu},K_{\nu}^{\prime}
, whose support is
\mathcal{B}_{1/5}
-avoiding and therefore recoverable. As in the proof of
Section

4.4
, the
X
terms in
K_{\mu}\mathsf{Z}^{u}
and
K_{\nu}^{\prime}\mathsf{Z}^{v}
can be chosen to be the same up to stabilizers.
Denote this
X
error
P_{X,s}
.
Similarly, the
Z
terms can be chosen to be the same up to stabilizers since
\mathsf{enc}(\outerproduct{0}{0}^{\otimes k})
is stabilized by all
Z
logical operators.
Denote this
Z
error
P_{Z,s}
.
We see that

\displaystyle\Pi_{s}\mathcal{E}(\outerproduct{0}{0}^{\otimes n})\Pi_{s}

\displaystyle\propto P_{X,s}P_{Z,s}\mathsf{enc}(\outerproduct{0}{0}^{\otimes k})P_{X,s}P_{Z,s}.

(6.100)

Therefore, the measured state is a linear combination of
\mathcal{B}_{1/5}
-avoiding
X
errors and arbitrarily supported
Z
errors acting on the ideal state
\mathsf{enc}(\outerproduct{0}{0}^{\otimes k})
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\circ\mathcal{E}(\outerproduct{0}{0}^{\otimes n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\text{syndrome }s}\outerproduct{s}{s}\otimes\left(P_{X,s}P_{Z,s}\mathsf{enc}(\outerproduct{0}{0}^{\otimes k})P_{X,s}P_{Z,s}\right)

(6.101)

From
Section

6.3
, we know that the error correction gadget is friendly in both bases. Since
X
errors have bounded support, the logical error resulting from the EC gadget is a
Z
error, which has no impact on the state
\mathsf{enc}(\outerproduct{0}{0}^{\otimes k})
.
We conclude that our initialization gadget has the correct output state and output code type.
∎

7
qLDPC Threshold Theorems

We first begin by proving a threshold theorem for the surface code roughly following the outline in
[
Den+02
]
.
The scheme we construct utilizes transversal gates and magic state distillation, and dose not limit the connectivity.
To prove a threshold under connectivity constraints (such as a 2D lattice), one could utilize physical
\mathsf{SWAP}
gates to route far-apart surface code patches near to each other (such as in
[
PKP23
]
) for transversal gates, or implement logical gates with lattice surgery (see
Section

6.3
).
Both of these approaches can be captured by our formalism, so it would be useful to add them.

7.1
Surface codes

We now begin a threshold proof using surface codes.
Here, we will introduce surface codes as a computational code as in
Section

6
.

{definition}

[Surface code]
For
d\in\mathbb{N}
, we use
\mathsf{SC}{({d})}
to refer to a surface code of distance
d
encoding one logical qubit into
O(d^{2})
physical qubits.
Concretely, we use the planar surface code (
[
BK98
,
FM01
]
) as defined in
[
Den+02
]
with parameters
\llbracket d^{2}+(d-1)^{2},1,d\rrbracket
.
For convenience,
\mathsf{SC}{({1})}
refers to the trivial quantum code on one qubit with identity encoding map.
We follow
Section

6.1
and let
\mathsf{SC}_{{c}}^{({d})}=(U,\mathcal{B}_{c})
denote the code types of a surface code of distance
d
.

{proposition}

A classical linear code defined by a check matrix
H\in\mathbb{F}^{r\times n}
with column weight at most 2 is efficiently minimum-weight decodable by the min-weight perfect matching decoder
[
Den+02
]
.

Proof sketch.

The proof is the standard construction of a matching decoder for the surface code.
We give a brief sketch here.
We introduce a graph
G_{\mathrm{decoding}}=([r]\cup\{b\},E)
with
r+1
vertices: One vertex for every row of
H
and then an additional “boundary vertex”
b
.
For every column of
H
introduce an edge in
G_{\mathrm{decoding}}
: If the column is weight two, connect the corresponding vertices
(r_{1},r_{2})
. If the column is weight one, connect the vertex to the boundary
(r_{1},b)
.

Given a syndrome
s\in\im H\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}^{r}\simeq\sigma\subseteq[r]
, add
b
to the set if
\sigma
is odd.
Then, we compute a minimum-weight perfect matching
M\subseteq\sigma\times\sigma
on the weighted fully connected graph
G_{\mathrm{matching}}=(\sigma,\sigma\times\sigma,w)
where
w(v_{1},v_{2})
is the weight of the shortest path connecting
v_{1},v_{2}
in
G_{\mathrm{decoding}}
.
The correction is the
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-sum of the shortest paths corresponding to each element of the matching
M
.
∎

{corollary}

[Surface code error correction gadget
[
Den+02
,
Kit03
,
BK98
,
Kit97
]
]
There is an error correction gadget
\mathsf{EC}^{{\mathsf{SC}{({d})}}}
(implementing identity) which has input code type
\mathsf{SC}_{{1/2}}^{({d})}
and output code type
\mathsf{SC}_{{1/5}}^{({d})}
.
Its family of bad fault paths has weight enumerator bounded as in
Eq.

6.62
.

Proof.

Consider the spacetime code studied in
Section

6.2
.
Its parity check matrix, illustrated in
Fig.

6
, has column weight bounded by 2 due to the structure of stabilizers in the surface code.
Therefore, minimum weight decoding of the spacetime code is equivalent to finding a min-weight perfect matching on a graph, which can be efficiently solved.
The rest of this lemma follows from
Section

6.3
.
∎

{lemma}

[Transversal Clifford gates]
There exists a constant
\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{TRANSVERSAL}}\in(0,1)
and friendly gate gadgets for the set of operations
\mathsf{TRANSVERSAL}=\{\mathsf{I},\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{CNOT},\mathsf{H},\mathsf{S},\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{X}},\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{Z}}\}
(and their classically controlled analogs, if unitary) such that

•

The gadgets for
\mathsf{I},\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{CNOT},\mathsf{H},\mathsf{S}
and their classically controlled analogs has input and output code types
\mathsf{SC}_{{1/5}}^{({d})}
. The gadgets for
\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{X}},\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{Z}}
has input code type
\mathsf{SC}_{{1/5}}^{({d})}
and trivial output code type.

•

Each gadget has width
O(d^{2})
and (identical) depth
O(d)
.

•

For each operation,
\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{TRANSVERSAL}
, the family of bad fault paths of the corresponding gadget
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{g}}
has weight enumerator bounded on
x\in[0,\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{TRANSVERSAL}}]
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{g}};~x\right)}\leq e^{-\beta d}\mathsf{poly}(d)

(7.1)

for some constant
\beta>0
.

Construction.
The logical operations
\mathsf{I},\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Z}
and
\mathsf{CNOT}
can all be implemented transversally on any CSS code.
A depth-2 implementation of
\mathsf{H}
applying
H^{\otimes n}
and one layer of
\mathsf{SWAP}
is given in
[
Mou16
,
BB24
]
, with the idea originating from
[
Den+02
]
.
A depth-1 implementation of
\mathsf{S}
applying a combination of
\mathsf{S}
and
\mathsf{CZ}
is given in
[
Mou16
,
BB24
]
.
The gate gadgets for these unitary gates are their low-depth implementation preceded by the EC gadget
\mathsf{EC}^{{\mathsf{SC}{({d})}}}
.
34
34

34

Technically, if the unitary implementation is depth-1, we add a layer of identity gates afterwards to make them depth-2 in order to satisfy the identical depth condition in lemma statement.

Gadgets for classically controlled analogs are constructed similarly.

For measurement
\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{X}}
, we first apply the EC gadget
\mathsf{EC}^{{\mathsf{SC}{({d})}}}
, then transversally measure all physical qubits of
\mathsf{SC}{({d})}
in
\mathsf{X}
basis to obtain a classical bitstring
b_{X}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
.
Let
H_{X}
denote the
\mathsf{X}
stabilizer check matrix of
\mathsf{SC}{({d})}
, compute
s_{X}=H_{X}b_{X}
, and apply a minimum weight decoder
D_{X}
to compute a correction
c_{X}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
where
H_{X}c_{X}=s_{X}
.
Let
L_{X}
be a
\mathsf{X}
logical operator of
\mathsf{SC}{({d})}
, and let
\ell_{X}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
be the indicator vector of its support. Output
\ell_{X}\cdot(b_{X}+c_{X})
. The gadget for
\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{Z}}
can be constructed analogously.
35
35

35

We pad these gadgets by identity gates (on classical registers) so that they have the same depth as the unitary gate gadgets.

\blacktriangleleft

Proof.

The unitary gate gadgets for
\{\mathsf{I},\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{CNOT},\mathsf{H},\mathsf{S}\}
have bad fault paths and weight enumerator bounds given by
Section

6.4
.

For
\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{Z}}
(and similarly
\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{X}}
), denote the proposed gadget
\mathsf{MEAS}_{\mathsf{Z}}
.
We first argue that in the absence of input error and fault, the perfect gadget
\mathsf{MEAS}_{\mathsf{Z}}
will correctly measure the logical qubit in
\mathsf{Z}
basis destructively.
To see that, since surface code is a CSS code, its logical state has a standard basis.

\displaystyle\forall v\in\ker(H_{Z}),\ket{v + H_X}

\displaystyle\propto\sum_{r\in\rowspan(H_{X})}\ket{v+r}.

(7.2)

Transversal measurement in the
\mathsf{Z}
basis will therefore measure to
v+r
for some
v\in\ker(H_{Z})
and
r\in\rowspan(H_{X})
.
We see that
H_{Z}(v+r)=0
, which means the decoder acts trivially.
For a
\mathsf{Z}
logical operator with indicator vector
\ell_{Z}\in\ker(H_{X})
, we see that
\ell_{Z}\cdot(v+r)=\ell_{Z}\cdot v
.
This is the correct logical measurement result due to the commutation rules of the logical operators of CSS codes.

In the presence of input error
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{in}}
and Pauli fault
\mathbf{f}
,
the fault
\mathbf{f}
is separable as
\mathbf{f}_{\mathsf{EC}}
supported on the locations of the EC gadget and
\mathbf{f}_{\mathsf{Z}}
supported on the locations of the transversal measurement.
Let us consider the output state of the EC gadget,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\circ\mathsf{enc}(\rho^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{map}[\mathsf{EC}^{{\mathsf{SC}{({d})}}}[\mathbf{f}_{\mathsf{EC}}]](\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{in}}\circ\mathsf{enc}(\rho)).

(7.3)

Assuming
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{in}}
and
\mathbf{f}
is well-behaving as in
Section

6.3
,
\rho^{\prime}=\rho
and
\mathcal{E}
is
\mathsf{SC}{({d})}_{1/5}
-avoiding.
Now consider the faulty transversal measurement circuit, which can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\mathsf{map}[\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{Z}}^{\otimes n}[\mathbf{f}_{\mathsf{Z}}]]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{f}_{\mathsf{Z},2}\circ\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{Z}}^{\otimes n}\circ\mathbf{f}_{\mathsf{Z},1}

(7.4)

for Pauli superoperators
\mathbf{f}_{\mathsf{Z},1},\mathbf{f}_{\mathsf{Z},2}
.
Note that the faults must respect the input and output types of the affected gates. Since the measurement gate output classical bits,
\mathbf{f}_{\mathsf{Z},2}
must be a diagonal Pauli fault which is a tensor product of
\mathsf{X}
operators acting on a collection of bits
F_{2}
.
To analyze
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathbf{f}_{\mathsf{Z},1}
, because we measured all physical qubits individually in the
\mathsf{Z}
basis, by the decoherence of errors lemma (
Section

4.4
)
\mathbf{f}_{\mathsf{Z},1}\circ\mathcal{E}
decoheres into single-qubit Pauli
\mathsf{X}
errors acting on a collection of qubits
F_{1}\cup E\subseteq[n]
, where
F_{1}
is a subset of the support of
\mathbf{f}_{\mathsf{Z},1}
and
E
is a subset of the support of
\mathcal{E}
.
36
36

36

Technically, we apply
Section

4.4
with the trivial quantum code whose stabilizer group consists of all
n
single-qubit
\mathsf{X}
operators.

Since the support of
\mathcal{E}
is
\mathsf{SC}{({d})}_{1/5}
-avoiding,
E
is
\mathsf{SC}{({d})}_{1/5}
-avoiding.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{Z}}}=\mathsf{SC}{({d})}_{1/5}
denote the bad fault paths on the transversal measurement.
If
\mathbf{f}_{\mathsf{Z}}
is
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{Z}}}
-avoiding, we see that
F=F_{1}\cup F_{2}
is
\mathsf{SC}{({d})}_{1/5}
-avoiding.
Let
1_{E},1_{F}
denote the indicator vectors for sets
E,F
, and let
e=1_{E}+1_{F}
.
We see that the support of
e
is
\mathsf{SC}{({d})}_{2/5}
-avoiding, and the measurement outcome is
v+r+e
.
Since we used a minimum weight decoder
D_{Z}
, the correction
c_{Z}
satisfies
c_{Z}+e\in\rowspan(H_{X})
(
Section

6.2
). Therefore,
v+r+e+c_{Z}\in v+\rowspan(H_{X})
and the output classical bit is again the correct logical measurement outcome.
We conclude that the proposed gadget fault-tolerantly implement
\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{Z}}
, with bad fault paths and weight enumerate bound given by
Section

6.4
.
∎

We remark that the transversal measurement gadgets can be straightforwardly generalized to any CSS code, measuring out all logical qubits in the
\mathsf{X}
or
\mathsf{Z}
basis.

7.1.1
State injection

To employ magic state distillation
[
BK05
]
, we need a source of noisy logical magic states.
Here, we will employ a brute-force approach to construct a state-injection gadget for the surface code.
We utilize the following unitary circuit for growing a surface code, which was presented in Figure 19 of
[
Den+02
]
.

{fact}

[Surface code growth]
For
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a depth
O(\ell)
unitary circuit
U_{(d+\ell)\leftarrow d}
that acts on a
\mathsf{SC}{({d})}
codeblock and
O(\ell d+\ell^{2})
qubits initialized to
\ket{0}
and returns a
\mathsf{SC}{({d+\ell})}
codeblock.
That is, as superoperators, we have that

\displaystyle U_{(d+\ell)\leftarrow d}\circ\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{SC}{({d})}}=\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{SC}{({d+\ell})}}~.

(7.5)

We can use the constant-depth circuit in
Section

7.1.1
alternately with the error correction gadget to prepare an arbitrarily large surface code block.
The main idea is that the error correction gadget has exponential error suppression in the block length, so there are relatively few ways that the earlier parts of this procedure can fail.

{lemma}
[Surface code state injection gadget
[
Den+02
]
]
There exists a constant
\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{INJECT}}\in(0,1)
such that,
given a constant-sized circuit
C
producing a state
\ket{\psi}
, there exists a gadget
\mathsf{INJECT}^{(d)}_{\ket{\psi}}
with output code type
\mathsf{SC}_{{1/5}}^{({d})}
that prepares
\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{SC}{({d})}}(\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi})
and bad fault paths
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{INJECT}}^{d}
with weight enumerator bounded on
x\in[0,\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{INJECT}}]
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{INJECT}}^{d};~x\right)}\leq c\cdot x

(7.6)

for some absolute constant
c>0
.
The depth is
O(d^{2})
and the width is
O(d^{2})
.

Construction.
Let
\mathsf{GROW}_{d^{\prime}\leftarrow d}
be the circuit that introduces the necessary ancilla qubits and applies the growth unitary
U_{d^{\prime}\leftarrow d}
from
Section

7.1.1
.
We define the
\mathsf{INJECT}^{(d)}
gadget inductively as

\displaystyle\mathsf{INJECT}^{(d)}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{GROW}_{d\leftarrow(d-1)}\circ\mathsf{EC}^{{\mathsf{SC}{({d-1})}}}\circ\mathsf{INJECT}^{(d-1)}

(7.7)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{GROW}_{d\leftarrow(d-1)}\circ\mathsf{EC}^{{\mathsf{SC}{({d-1})}}}\circ\dots\circ\mathsf{GROW}_{3\leftarrow 2}\circ\mathsf{EC}^{{\mathsf{SC}{({2})}}}\circ\mathsf{GROW}_{2\leftarrow 1}~.

(7.8)

This is a gadget for identity. The gadget that injects a given state
\ket{\psi}
is simply the circuit
C
followed by injection.

\displaystyle\mathsf{INJECT}^{(d)}_{\ket{\psi}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{INJECT}^{(d)}\circ C.

(7.9)

\blacktriangleleft

Proof.

For a code distance
s
, we analyze the gadget
\mathsf{GROW}_{s+1\leftarrow s}\circ\mathsf{EC}^{{\mathsf{SC}{({s})}}}
.
Since the growth circuit from
Section

7.1.1
has constant depth, by
Section

6.4
, this is a friendly gadget that implements identity with input code type
\mathsf{SC}_{{1/5}}^{({s})}
and output code type
\mathsf{SC}_{{1/5}}^{({s+1})}
, with bad fault paths
\mathcal{F}_{s}
.

We now define the bad fault paths
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{INJECT}}^{d}
.
Let
\bar{d}
be a absolute constant to be specified later.
Let
V
be the set of all locations in the circuit
\mathsf{INJECT}^{(\bar{d})}
. Define
\mathcal{F}_{1}
to be all singletons of
V
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{\{v\}:v\in V\}.

(7.10)

Since
\bar{d}
is a constant, the number of locations in
V
is also a constant.
For
s\geq\bar{d}
, recall that
\mathcal{F}_{s}
is defined by
Section

6.4
.
We define
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{INJECT}}^{d}
to be the sum of all of these fault paths where
\mathcal{F}_{s}
should be understood as subsets of locations of the
s
-th step in the growth sequence and
\mathcal{F}_{1}
as subsets of locations of the first
\bar{d}
-steps in the growth sequence.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{INJECT}}^{d}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{1}\boxplus_{s=\bar{d}}^{d}\mathcal{F}_{s}.

(7.11)

Intuitively, this definition enforces that the injection gadget suffers no faults until the surface code size reaches a large enough constant, after which the exponential error suppression of the EC gadgets will dominate and ensure that the weight enumerator is sufficiently bounded.

We proceed to the analysis. From
Section

6.4
, there exists a polynomial
\mathsf{poly}
and absolute constants
c_{1},c_{2}
such that the weight enumerator of
\mathcal{F}_{s}
can be bounded as follows.

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{s};~x\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{poly}(s)(c_{1}x^{1/c_{2}})^{s}.

(7.12)

We want to restrict
x
and
s
such that the following inequalities hold

\displaystyle\mathsf{poly}(s)(c_{1}x^{1/c_{2}})^{s}\leq 2^{-s}x^{\frac{s}{2c_{2}}}\leq 2^{-s}x.

(7.13)

Note that the first inequality is equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathsf{poly}(s)(2c_{1}x^{\frac{1}{2c_{2}}})^{s}\leq 1.

(7.14)

Let
\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{INJECT}}=(\frac{1}{4c_{1}})^{2c_{2}}
.
For all
x\in[0,\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{INJECT}})
, it holds that
2c_{1}x^{\frac{1}{2c_{2}}}<1/2
.
Now let be
\bar{d}
an integer which is at least
2c_{2}
, such that for all
s\geq\bar{d}
, it holds that
\mathsf{poly}(s)2^{-s}\leq 1
.
Then
Eq.

7.14
holds, which implies
Eq.

7.13
.
The weight enumerator can then be bounded using the preceding equations.

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{INJECT}}^{d};~x\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{1};~x\right)}+\sum_{s=\bar{d}}^{d}\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{s};~x\right)}

(7.15)

\displaystyle\leq|V|\cdot x+\sum_{s=\bar{d}}^{d}\mathsf{poly}(s)(c_{1}x^{1/c_{2}})^{s}

(7.16)

\displaystyle\leq|V|\cdot x+\sum_{s=\bar{d}}^{d}2^{-s}x

(7.17)

\displaystyle\leq(|V|+1)\cdot x.

(7.18)

The depth of the gadget is
O(d^{2})
since every error correction gadget has depth bounded by
O(d)
and every growth circuit has constant depth. This completes the analysis of
\mathsf{INJECT}^{(d)}
.

For a given state
\ket{\psi}
produced by a constant-sized circuit
C
, let
V_{C}
be the set of locations of
C
, and define
\mathcal{F}_{C}
to be all singletons of
V
.
The collection of bad fault paths for
\mathsf{INJECT}^{(d)}_{\ket{\psi}}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{INJECT},\ket{\psi}}^{d}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{INJECT}}^{d}\boxplus\mathcal{F}_{C},

(7.19)

and its weight enumerator is bounded by
(|V|+|V_{C}|+1)\cdot x
.
∎

In practice, it is better to grow the lattice all at once
[
Li15
]
, but the analysis of the lattice growth leads to some involved combinatorics.
Interested readers should refer to
[
od+15
,
SBB23
]
.

7.1.2
Magic state distillation

We will use the magic states
\ket{\mathsf{T}}\equiv\mathsf{T}\ket{+}
.

{fact}

There exists a quantum CSS code
{\mathcal{Q}^{(\mathrm{distill})}}
with parameters
\llbracket 15,1,3\rrbracket
such that the encoding map is implementable by a Clifford circuit and transversal
\mathsf{T}
applies logical
\mathsf{T}
.
That is,

\displaystyle\mathsf{dec}_{{\mathcal{Q}^{(\mathrm{distill})}}}\mathsf{T}^{\otimes 15}\mathsf{enc}_{{\mathcal{Q}^{(\mathrm{distill})}}}=\mathsf{T}~.

(7.20)

{fact}

[
\mathsf{T}
teleportation circuit]
It is a standard result that
\ket{\mathsf{T}}
can be consumed by a Clifford circuit containing a classically-controlled
\mathsf{SX}
operation in order to implement
\mathsf{T}
. For reference, see Section 10.6.2, Figure 10.25 of
[
NC12
]
.

{proposition}

[Single-level state distillation]
Let
\mathcal{S}_{(2,15)}
be all size-2 subsets of the set
[15]
.
There exists a constant-sized Clifford circuit with measurements and classical processing acting on a
15
-qubit state such that when the input is
\mathcal{S}_{(2,15)}
-deviated from
\outerproduct{\mathsf{T}}{\mathsf{T}}^{\otimes 15}
, the output is proportional to (trivially deviated from)
\outerproduct{\mathsf{T}}{\mathsf{T}}
.

Construction.
The circuit consists of four steps.

1.

\mathsf{PREP}
: Prepare the logical
\ket{+}
state of the code
{\mathcal{Q}^{(\mathrm{distill})}}
from
Section

7.1.2
.

2.

\mathsf{TP}
: Use the teleportation circuit from
Section

7.1.2
to apply
\mathsf{T}
to the logical
\ket{+}
state.

3.

\mathsf{EC}_{\mathrm{distill}}
: Perform syndrome extraction and apply a minimum weight correction.

4.

Apply
\mathsf{dec}_{\mathrm{distill}}
and output the remaining qubit.

\blacktriangleleft

Proof.

We assumed that the circuit is executed without fault and the input is
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{in}}(\outerproduct{\mathsf{T}}{\mathsf{T}}^{\otimes 15})
, where
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{in}}
is supported on a single-qubit.
Consider the state

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle=\mathsf{TP}\circ\mathsf{PREP}\circ\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{in}}(\outerproduct{\mathsf{T}}{\mathsf{T}}^{\otimes 15})

(7.21)

The error
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{in}}
is propagated to an error
\mathcal{E}
acting on one of the qubits of
{\mathcal{Q}^{(\mathrm{distill})}}
, while the remaining 14 qubits have the correct
\mathsf{T}
gates applied.

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathsf{T}^{\otimes 14})\circ\mathsf{enc}_{\mathrm{distill}}(\outerproduct{+}{+})

(7.22)

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{E}\circ\mathsf{T}^{\dagger})\circ\mathsf{T}^{\otimes 15}\circ\mathsf{enc}_{\mathrm{distill}}(\outerproduct{+}{+})

(7.23)

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{E}\circ\mathsf{T}^{\dagger})\circ\mathsf{enc}_{\mathrm{distill}}(\outerproduct{\mathsf{T}}{\mathsf{T}})

(7.24)

Upon application of
\mathsf{EC}_{\mathrm{distill}}
, by the decoherence of errors lemma (
Section

4.4
), the error superoperator
\mathcal{E}\circ\mathsf{T}^{\dagger}
decomposes into a linear combination of (diagonal) Pauli errors supported on the affected qubit, which is then corrected by the minimum weight correction.

\displaystyle\mathsf{EC}_{\mathrm{distill}}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\propto\mathsf{enc}_{\mathrm{distill}}(\outerproduct{\mathsf{T}}{\mathsf{T}})

(7.25)

Therefore applying
\mathsf{dec}_{\mathrm{distill}}
gives us the desired
\outerproduct{\mathsf{T}}{\mathsf{T}}
state.
∎

{lemma}

[Logical level state distillation gadget
[
BK05
]
]
Let
\mathsf{PREPARE}_{\ket{T}}
be the superoperator that outputs a
\ket{T}
state.
There exists a constant
\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{PREPARE}}\in(0,1)
such that for
d\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a family of gadgets
\mathsf{\overline{PREPARE}}^{(d)}_{\ket{T}}
for
\mathsf{PREPARE}_{\ket{T}}
that has output code type
\mathsf{SC}_{{1/5}}^{({d})}
and a family of bad fault paths
\mathcal{F}_{d}^{\mathsf{\overline{PREPARE}}_{\ket{T}}}
satisfying the upper bound

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{d}^{\mathsf{\overline{PREPARE}}_{\ket{T}}};~x\right)}=O\left(\mathsf{poly}(d)\cdot e^{-\beta d}\right)

(7.26)

on
x\in[0,\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{PREPARE}}]
.

\mathsf{\overline{PREPARE}}^{(d)}
has width
O(d^{2+\log_{2}(15)})
and depth
O(d^{2})
.

Construction.
We fix a constant
\ell=\lceil\log_{2}\beta d\rceil
corresponding to the number of rounds of distillation.
Run the state injection gadget
\mathsf{INJECT}^{(d)}_{\ket{\mathsf{T}}}
(
Section

7.1.1
) for the circuit
\mathsf{T}\ket{+}
in parallel
N_{0}=15^{\ell}
times followed by the error correction gadget
37
37

37

The EC gadget is not necessary, but we perform it for conceptual clarity.

\mathsf{EC}^{{\mathsf{SC}{({d})}}}
to obtain the state
\rho_{0}
which is a noisy copy of
\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{SC}{({d})}}(\outerproduct{\mathsf{T}}{\mathsf{T}})^{\otimes N_{0}}
.
Let
\mathsf{DISTILL}
be the distillation circuit for
\ket{T}
(
Section

7.1.2
) where every gate is replaced by
\mathsf{SC}{({d})}
transversal gate gadgets (
Section

7.1
).
Perform
\ell
rounds of the following procedure to obtain the sequence of states
\rho_{1},\dots\rho_{\ell}
:

\mathsf{DISTILL}

15^{\ell-i}
times in parallel on

to obtain
\rho_{i}
.
It follows that
\rho_{i}
is
N_{i}=15^{\ell-i-1}
noisy copies of
\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{SC}{({d})}}(\outerproduct{\mathsf{T}}{\mathsf{T}})
.
Output
\rho_{\ell}
.

\blacktriangleleft

Proof.

\mathsf{INJECT}^{(d)}_{\ket{\mathsf{T}}}
has depth
O(d^{2})
while the transversal gate gadgets have depth
O(d)
. There are
O(\ell)
rounds of distillation, each one having
O(d)
depth, so the overall depth is
O(d^{2}+\ell\cdot d)=O(d^{2})
.
Overall there are at most
O(15^{\ell})
gadgets at any time, each of width
O(d^{2})
, so the total width is
O\left(d^{2+\log_{2}(15)}\right)
.

Weight enumerators

Let
\Omega_{\mathsf{INJECT}}
be the set of
\mathsf{INJECT}^{(d)}_{\ket{\mathsf{T}}}
gadgets and
\Omega_{\mathsf{TRANSVERSAL}}
the set of all error correction and transversal gate gadgets in the distillation steps (that is, including the error correction gadget after the distillation gadget but excluding any error correction gadgets within the injection gadgets).
Let
\Omega_{\mathsf{DISTILL}}
be all instances of the distillation circuit
\mathsf{DISTILL}
.
The gadget will have two families of bad fault paths corresponding to failure of too many injection gadgets
\mathcal{F}_{1}
and failure of a transversal gate of the distillation circuit
\mathcal{F}_{2}
, respectively.

\mathcal{F}_{2}
will be the sum of the families of bad faults associated with each transversal gate gadget.
In the following expression, we use
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{g}}
to mean the family of bad fault paths associated with the transversal gate gadgets
\mathsf{g}\in\Omega_{\mathsf{TRANSVERSAL}}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{2}=\wtsum_{\mathsf{g}\in\Omega_{\mathsf{TRANSVERSAL}}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{g}}

(7.27)

There are
O(\ell~15^{\ell})
transversal gate gadgets, so utilizing the bound in
Section

7.1
, on values of
x\in[0,\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{TRANSVERSAL}}]
, the weight enumerator of
\mathcal{F}_{2}
obeys the bound

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{2};~x\right)}\leq O(\ell~15^{\ell}~\mathsf{poly}(d)~e^{-\beta d})

(7.28)

Let
\mathcal{S}_{(2,15)}
be all size-2 subsets of
[15]
.
For

, the code blocks of
\rho_{i}
are in bijection with the
(\ell-i)
-fold Cartesian product
[15]^{\times(\ell-i)}
.
For conciseness, we define

(singleton set) and
()\in\{\varnothing\}
(empty tuple).
The distillation circuits
\Omega_{\mathsf{DISTILL}}
are in bijection with

such that the distillation circuit at coordinates

consumes the code blocks with coordinates
(i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell-i})\times[15]
.
Let

be the set (recall the definition of

from

)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\underbrace{\mathcal{S}_{(2,15)}\bullet\dots\bullet\mathcal{S}_{(2,15)}}_{\ell\text{ times}}~.

(7.29)

That is, for an

-avoiding set

and every

-avoiding on every

of

-rectangles.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{INJECT}}

.
We will define

-avoiding.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{1}=\mathcal{S}\bullet\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{INJECT}}

(7.30)

Using the composition evaluation formula (

) and the simple weight enumerator
\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{S}_{(2,15)};~x\right)}=105x^{2}
, a short calculation
38
38

38

For a function

, the
\ell
-fold composition
f^{(\ell)}(x)
obeys the recursion

.
shows that the weight enumerator of

is

(7.31)

Thus, on

can be bounded as

for some constant

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{1};~x\right)}\leq e^{-2^{\ell}}~.

(7.34)

The family of bad fault paths for the gadget is the sum

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{d}_{\mathsf{\overline{PREPARE}}_{\ket{T}}}=\mathcal{F}_{1}\wtsum\mathcal{F}_{2}~.

(7.35)

Using the choice of

\displaystyle=O\left(\mathsf{poly}(d)e^{-\beta d}\right)~.

Correctness

It remains to show that the output satisfies the code type
\mathsf{SC}_{{1/5}}^{({d})}
when the fault path is

Let

be a

-avoiding Pauli fault.
Recall that
\rho_{0}
is the state after state injection and error correction

.

From the construction of

is

-avoiding and
\mathcal{F}_{2}
-avoiding.
It follows that there is an

-deviated from

.
Using the friendliness property

39

39

\mathcal{S}
-avoiding set are arbitrarily damaged and are corrected to some arbitrary codestate.
of the error correction gadgets, it follows that

where

-deviated from
\outerproduct{\mathsf{T}}{\mathsf{T}}^{\otimes{N_{0}}}
.

\displaystyle\rho_{0}

\displaystyle\precapprox_{\mathsf{SC}_{{1/5}}^{({d})}}\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{SC}{({d})}}(\bar{\rho}_{0})

(7.38)

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}_{0}

\displaystyle\precapprox_{\mathcal{S}}\outerproduct{\mathsf{T}}{\mathsf{T}}^{\otimes{N_{0}}}

(7.39)

Using the property that
\mathcal{F}_{2}
is the sum of the bad fault paths for each transversal gate gadget of

and applying the gate the gadget definition (

Consider layer-
i

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathsf{DISTILL}}^{(i)}\equiv[15]^{\times(\ell-i)}\subseteq\Omega_{\mathsf{DISTILL}}.

\Omega_{\mathsf{DISTILL}}^{(i-1)}

or
(j_{1},\dots,j_{\ell-i})\in I
.
Then, applying

7.1.3
Assembly

{theorem}

[Threshold theorem for surface code quantum computation]
There exists a constant
\epsilon_{*}\in(0,1)
such that, for any Clifford+T circuit
C
of width
W
and depth
D
and any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists a circuit
\overline{C}
that is a fault-tolerant gadget for
C
with bad fault paths
\mathcal{F}
.
When
C
is a circuit with only classical inputs and classical outputs,
\overline{C}
has trivial input and output types.
The type of any quantum inputs and outputs is
\mathsf{SC}_{{1/5}}^{({d})}
where
d=O(\log(V)\mathrm{polyloglog}(V))
.

Let
V=\frac{WD}{\epsilon}
.
Then,
\overline{C}
has width
\overline{W}
and depth
\overline{D}
satisfying the bounds

\displaystyle\overline{W}

\displaystyle=O(W\log^{5.91}(V)\mathrm{polyloglog}(V))

(7.42)

\displaystyle\overline{D}

\displaystyle=O(D\log^{2}(V)\mathrm{polyloglog}(V))~.

(7.43)

On
x\in[0,\epsilon_{*}]
, the weight enumerator of
\mathcal{F}
satisfies the bound

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F};~x\right)}\leq\epsilon~.

(7.44)

Construction.
We will defer the choice of the surface code size
d\in\mathbb{N}
to the proof.
Let
\epsilon_{*}=\min\left(\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{PREPARE}},\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{TRANSVERSAL}}\right)
.

C
is Clifford+T, at the loss of a constant multiplicative factor in the depth, we can assume it contains gates and their classically controlled analogs (except for measurement) from the following set

\displaystyle\mathsf{GATESET}=\{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{I},\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{CNOT},\mathsf{H},\mathsf{S},\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{X}},\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{Z}},\mathsf{INIT}_{\mathsf{Z}}\}~.

(7.45)

We begin by construction a set of gadgets that implement gates in
\mathsf{GATESET}
and have identical depth.
We construct a gadget
\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{T}}
for
\mathsf{T}
in the following way: First prepare a magic state with the gadget
\mathsf{\overline{PREPARE}}^{(d)}_{\ket{T}}
(
Section

7.1.2
), then consume it to apply a teleported
\mathsf{T}
gate using the transversal gate gadgets (
Section

7.1
) implementing the
\mathsf{T}
teleportation circuit (
Section

7.1.2
).
Let
\tau
be the duration of this gadget.

For all other gates
\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{GATESET}\setminus\{\mathsf{T}\}
, the operation is in
\mathsf{TRANSVERSAL}
.
Therefore, we can construct the corresponding gadget
\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{g}}
by padding gadgets from
Section

7.1
to length
\tau
with an additional error correction gadget.

Construct the family of circuits
\overline{C}_{d}
by replacing every gate
\mathsf{g}
of
C
with the corresponding gadget
\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{g}}
.

\blacktriangleleft

Proof.

First, since
\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{T}}
is the composition of a constant number of gadgets with the state preparation gadget,
\tau=O(d^{2})
(i.e. the logical cycle time is not too long).
Consider an operation
\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{GATESET}
and let
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{g}}
be the weight enumerator for the gadget
\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{g}}
.
Applying the weight enumerator upper bounds (
Section

7.1
,
Section

7.1.2
), it follows that the previously constructed gadgets are
(\mathsf{g},\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{g}})
-FT gadgets in the sense of
Section

4.1
where for
x\in[0,\epsilon_{*}]
, the weight enumerator of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{g}}
is bounded as

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{g}};~x\right)}\leq\mathsf{poly}(d)e^{-\beta d}~.

(7.46)

Let
\bar{V}
be the set of gadgets of
\overline{C}_{d}
.
For each gadget
g\in\bar{V}
of
\overline{C}_{d}
, let
\mathcal{F}_{g}
be its bad fault paths.
We define the bad fault paths of
\overline{C}_{d}
to

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{d}=\boxplus_{g\in\bar{V}}\mathcal{F}_{g}~.

(7.47)

It follows from the construction and the application of
Section

4.1
inductively on a topological sort of the bundled circuit corresponding to
\overline{C}_{d}
that
\overline{C}_{d}
is a
(C,\mathcal{U}_{d})
-FT gadget.
Using the weight enumerator upper bounds and
Section

2
, it follows that for
x\in[0,\epsilon_{*}]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{U}_{d};~x\right)}\leq\bar{V}\mathsf{poly}(d)e^{-\beta d}~.

(7.48)

Since
\bar{V}=O(WD)
, there exists
d=O(\log(V)~\mathrm{polyloglog}(V))
such that
\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{U}_{d};~x\right)}\leq\epsilon
.

\overline{C}
is
\overline{C}_{d}
with the minimum such choice of
d
.

By using the depth and width bounds of the corresponding gadgets, depth
\overline{D}
and width
\overline{W}
of
\overline{C}
satisfies

\displaystyle\overline{D}=O(D\tau)

\displaystyle=O(D\log(V)^{2}~\mathrm{polyloglog}(V))

(7.49)

\displaystyle\overline{W}=O(Wd^{2+\log_{2}(15)})

\displaystyle=O(W\log(V)^{5.91}~\mathrm{polyloglog}(V))~.\qed

(7.50)

{remark}

[Spacetime overhead]
It is possible to obtain a depth overhead of
\widetilde{O}(\log(V))
by growing the lattice all at once
[
Den+02
,
Li15
]
, but this requires some more complicated combinatorics.
It is possible to reduce the width overhead to
\widetilde{O}(\log^{2+\gamma}(V))
where
\gamma
is the exponent of the magic state distillation scheme distillation cost by using a different magic state distillation schemes (e.g. with post selection or different codes).
For example, it is possible to achieve
\gamma\leq 2.47

[
BK05
]
,
\gamma\leq 1.6

[
BH12
]
,
\gamma\leq 0.68

[
HH18
]
.
For states different from
\ket{T}
but convertible to
\ket{T}
with a catalyst state
[
Bev+20
]
, it is possible to achieve
\gamma=o_{d\to\infty}(1)
without substantially harming the time complexity
[
WHY24
,
GG25
,
Ngu25
,
NP24
]
.

A direct corollary of the above theorem is that we can obtain a surface code fault tolerance scheme against local stochastic noise with a constant threshold.
Using
Section

5.3
, we can also obtain a fault tolerance scheme against coherent noise, albeit the threshold is sub-constant.

{corollary}

[Sub-constant threshold against coherent noise] There exists a polylogarithmically decaying function
\delta_{*}=O(1/\log^{6.91}(WD/\epsilon))
such that, for any Clifford+T circuit
C
of width
W
and depth
D
and any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there exists a circuit
\overline{C}
that is a fault-tolerant gadget for
C
, whose parameters are given in
Section

7.1.3
.
When
C
is a circuit with classical inputs and classical outputs,
\overline{C}
has trivial input and output types.
The outputs of
C
and
\overline{C}
are
\epsilon
-close in trace distance when
\overline{C}
is subject to coherent noise
\mathbf{f}
of strength at most
\delta_{*}
.

Proof.

The construction is given in
Section

7.1.3
, and the proof is almost the same except we use
Section

5.3
to turn the upper bound on the weight enumerator of the bad fault paths into a bound on the trace distance.
Note that the condition in
Section

5.3
, which asks that the bad fault paths of the whole circuit is a sum of bad fault paths of individual gadgets, is satisfied.
The number of gadgets
m
satisfies
m\leq WD
.
We want

\displaystyle m(1+\eta)^{m}\eta\leq\epsilon,

(7.51)

where
\eta=\max_{i\in m}\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{i};~2(1+x)^{\ratio-1}x\right)}
, and is defined as

\displaystyle\ratio=\max_{i\in[m]}\max_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{i}}|V_{i}|/|F|,

(7.52)

for
V_{i}
being the set of locations in the
i
-th gadget.

To satisfy
Eq.

7.51
, it suffices for us to choose
\eta<\frac{\epsilon}{2m}=O(\epsilon/WD)
, as that would imply

\displaystyle m(1+\eta)^{m}\eta

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon e^{\eta m}/2\leq\epsilon e^{\epsilon/2}/2\leq\epsilon.

(7.53)

According to
Section

7.1
and
Section

7.1.2
, the gadgets in the circuit has bad fault paths
\mathcal{F}_{i}
such that for
x\in[0,\epsilon_{*}]
, the weight enumerator is bounded as

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{i};~x\right)}\leq\mathsf{poly}(d)e^{-\beta d}

(7.54)

for some absolute constant
\beta
.
41
41

41

Technically, the constant
\beta
depends on the value of
x
, but we can always take the minimal value of
\beta
for this upper bound.

Let us choose
d=O(WD/\epsilon)
such that

\displaystyle\eta<\frac{\epsilon}{2m}\leq\mathsf{poly}(d)e^{-\beta d}.

(7.55)

It suffices for us to choose
\delta_{*}
such that for all
x\leq\delta_{*}
, we have

\displaystyle 2(1+x)^{\ratio-1}x\leq\epsilon_{*}.

(7.56)

From
Section

7.1
and
Section

7.1.2
, we know that the volume of each gadget is at most
O(d^{7.91})
, and the bad sets are all of weight at least
\Omega(d)
.
This implies that
\ratio=O(d^{6.91})
.
Choose
\delta_{*}=\frac{\epsilon_{*}\log\ratio}{2\ratio}
, then for all
x\leq\delta_{*}
, it holds that

\displaystyle 2(1+x)^{\ratio-1}x\leq\epsilon_{*}e^{\ratio x}/\ratio\leq\epsilon_{*}e^{\epsilon_{*}\log\ratio/2}/\ratio\leq\epsilon_{*}.

(7.57)

The threshold delays polylogarithmically in the circuit volume, namely,
\delta_{*}=O(\log d/d^{6.91})
where
d=O(WD/\epsilon)
.
∎

7.2
Fault-tolerant quantum output

{lemma}

[Surface code unencoding gadget]
There exists a constant
\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{UNENCODE}}\in(0,1)
such that we have a gadget
\mathsf{UNENCODE}^{(d)}
for identity with input code type
\mathsf{SC}_{{1/5}}^{({d})}
and trivial output code type and bad fault paths
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{UNENCODE}}^{d}
with weight enumerator bounded on
x\in[0,\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{UNENCODE}}]
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{UNENCODE}}^{d};~x\right)}\leq c\cdot x

(7.58)

for some absolute constant
c>0
.
The depth is
O(d^{2})
and the width is
O(d^{2})
.

Construction.
Recall the growth unitary
U_{d^{\prime}\leftarrow d}
from
Section

7.1.1
, let
\mathsf{SHRINK}_{d^{\prime}\rightarrow d}
be the channel which applies the inverse unitary
U_{d^{\prime}\leftarrow d}^{\dagger}
and traces out the unencoded ancilla qubits. We define the unencoding gadget by alternatively apply
\mathsf{SHRINK}
and
\mathsf{EC}
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{UNENCODE}^{(d)}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{SHRINK}_{d\rightarrow d-1}\circ\mathsf{EC}^{{\mathsf{SC}{({d-1})}}}\circ\mathsf{UNENCODE}^{(d-1)}

(7.59)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{SHRINK}_{d\rightarrow d-1}\circ\mathsf{EC}^{{\mathsf{SC}{({d-1})}}}\circ\dots\circ\mathsf{SHRINK}_{3\rightarrow 2}\circ\mathsf{EC}^{{\mathsf{SC}{({2})}}}\circ\mathsf{SHRINK}_{2\rightarrow 1}

(7.60)

\blacktriangleleft

Proof.

Note that
\mathsf{UNENCODE}^{(d)}
is defined analogously to the injection gadget
\mathsf{INJECT}^{(d)}
, with the growth unitary inverted.
The definition and analysis for its bad fault paths follow the same arguments as in
Section

7.1.1
.
∎

{lemma}

[Quantum output]
There exists a constant
\epsilon_{*}\in(0,1)
such that, for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and any Clifford+T circuit
C
of width
W
and depth
D
outputting
n
qubits, there exists a circuit
\overline{C}
with trivial input and output types.
For any family of bad error supports
\mathcal{U}\subseteq P([n])
, there is a collection of bad fault paths
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{U}}
such that for any
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{U}}
-avoding Pauli fault
\mathbf{f}
, for any input state
\rho
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{map}[\overline{C}[\mathbf{f}]](\rho)

\displaystyle\precapprox_{\mathcal{U}}\mathsf{map}[C](\rho).

(7.61)

Let
V=\frac{WD}{\epsilon}
.
Then,
\overline{C}
has width
\overline{W}
and depth
\overline{D}
satisfying the bounds

\displaystyle\overline{W}

\displaystyle=O(W\log^{5.91}(V)\mathrm{polyloglog}(V))

(7.62)

\displaystyle\overline{D}

\displaystyle=O(D\log^{2}(V)\mathrm{polyloglog}(V))~.

(7.63)

On
x\in[0,\epsilon_{*}]
, the weight enumerator of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{U}}
satisfies the bound

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{U}};~x\right)}\leq\epsilon+\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{U};~c\cdot x\right)}

(7.64)

where
c>0
is an absolute constant.

Construction.
Apply
Section

7.1.3
with circuit
C
to get a fault-tolerant gadget
C_{\mathsf{FT}}
for
C
with bad fault paths
\mathcal{F}
.
Let
d
be the distance value chosen in
Section

7.1.3
.
Apply the unencoding gadget
\mathsf{UNENCODE}^{(d)}
to all the output logical qubits of
C_{\mathsf{FT}}
to obtain the circuit
\overline{C}
.

\displaystyle\overline{C}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathsf{UNENCODE}^{(d)}\right)^{\otimes n}\circ C_{\mathsf{FT}}

(7.65)

\blacktriangleleft

Proof.

Recall that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{UNENCODE}}^{d}
is the bad fault paths of the unencoding gadget
\mathsf{UNENCODE}^{(d)}
.
We define the bad fault paths of
\overline{C}
to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{U}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}\boxplus(\mathcal{U}\bullet\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{UNENCODE}}^{d}).

(7.66)

The upper bound on weight enumerator follows from
Section

2
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{U}};~x\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F};~x\right)}+\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{U};~c\cdot x\right)}

(7.67)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon+\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{U};~c\cdot x\right)}

(7.68)

For correctness, decompose
\mathbf{f}
into
\mathbf{f}_{1}
, which is supported on the locations of
C_{\mathsf{FT}}
and
\mathbf{f}_{2}
, which is supported on the locations of the unencoding gadgets.
From
Section

7.1.3
, we know that
C_{\mathsf{FT}}
is a fault-tolerant gadget for
C
with output type
\mathsf{SC}{({d})}_{1/5}
on every output logical qubit.
Let
\sigma
be the output state of
C
given input
\rho
, then on every surface code block, the output state
\bar{\sigma}:=\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{FT}}[\mathbf{f}_{1}]](\rho)
is
\mathsf{SC}{({d})}_{1/5}
-deviated from
\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{SC}{({d})}}^{\otimes n}(\sigma)
.
Notationally, we write

\displaystyle\forall i\in[n],\bar{\sigma}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{i}\circ\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{SC}{({d})}}(\sigma_{i})

(7.69)

for some
\mathsf{SC}{({d})}_{1/5}
-avoiding superoperator error
\mathcal{E}_{i}
.

Now let
F\subseteq[n]
denote the set of unencoding gadgets
\mathsf{UNENCODE}^{(d)}
on which the fault
\mathbf{f}_{2}
is not
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{UNENCODE}}^{d}
-avoiding.
From the definition of composition (the
\bullet
operation), we see that
F
is
\mathcal{U}
-avoiding.
For all
i\notin F
, the
i
-th unencoding gadget correctly implements identity. For
i\in F
, let
\mathbf{f}_{2,i}
denote the restriction of
\mathbf{f}_{2}
to the locations of the
i
-th unencoding gadget, and define
\mathcal{N}_{i}:=\mathsf{map}[\mathsf{UNENCODE}^{(d)}[\mathbf{f}_{2,i}]]
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{map}[\overline{C}[\mathbf{f}]](\rho)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{map}[\left(\mathsf{UNENCODE}^{(d)}\right)^{\otimes n}[\mathbf{f}_{2}]]\circ\mathsf{map}[C_{\mathsf{FT}}[\mathbf{f}_{1}]](\rho)

(7.70)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{map}[\left(\mathsf{UNENCODE}^{(d)}\right)^{\otimes n}[\mathbf{f}_{2}]]\circ\left(\bigotimes_{i\in[n]}\mathcal{E}_{i}\circ\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{SC}{({d})}}\right)(\sigma)

(7.71)

\displaystyle=\left(\bigotimes_{i\notin F}\mathsf{dec}_{\mathsf{SC}{({d})}}\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}\circ\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{SC}{({d})}}\right)\otimes\left(\bigotimes_{i\in F}\mathcal{N}_{i}\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}\circ\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{SC}{({d})}}\right)(\sigma)

(7.72)

\displaystyle=\left(\bigotimes_{i\in F}\mathcal{N}_{i}\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}\circ\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{SC}{({d})}}\right)(\sigma).

(7.73)

Since
F
is
\mathcal{U}
-avoiding, we see that
\mathsf{map}[\overline{C}[\mathbf{f}]](\rho)\precapprox_{U}\sigma
.
The bounds on the width and depth of
\overline{C}
follows a simple calculation from
Section

7.1.3
and
Section

7.2
.
∎

7.3
Constant space overhead fault-tolerance

Having constructed a fault-tolerance scheme with space and time overhead, we can use it to work our way up to a scheme with constant quantum space overhead
[
Got14
,
FGL20
,
TKY24
,
NP24
]
.
Here, we follow roughly the construction of
[
TKY24
]
with surface codes replacing concatenated codes and non-single-shot error correction gadgets instead of single-short ones.
It should be noted that the theorem proved in
[
TKY24
]
is stronger in that the model of computation here does not restrict the size of the classical compution: Effectively, we have assumed the existence of a classical minimum-weight decoder which runs instantaneously.
The exponent of the depth overhead in
Section

7.3
can be reduced to
1+o(1)
in a model of computation with very limited or even constant depth classical computation
[
NP24
]
at the cost of a substantially more involved construction.

We first need the following fact about the existence of an asympotically good qLDPC code family shown by
[
PK22
]
, building upton a line of work
[
HHO21
,
PK22a
,
BE21
]
.
The work of
[
PK22
]
is later extended by
[
LZ22
,
Din+23
]
.

{fact}

[Good qLDPC codes]
There exists an efficiently constructable qLDPC code family indexed by
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that for some absolute constant
c>1
, the
i
-th member of the family
\mathsf{qLDPC}({i})
has parameters
n_{i}=\Theta(c^{i})
,

\displaystyle\llbracket n_{i},\Theta(n_{i}),\Theta(n_{i})\rrbracket

(7.74)

{definition}

[Good qLDPC code family]
We use
\mathsf{qLDPC}_{{c}}^{({i})}
to refer to the code type constructed from the
i
-th member of the family of
Section

7.3
with the encoding map and bad sets constructed as in
Section

7.1
.

{proposition}

[Transversal Clifford gates for CSS qLDPC codes]
There exists a constant
\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{qLDPCTRANSVERSAL}}\in(0,1)
and friendly gate gadgets for the set of operations

\displaystyle\mathsf{CSSTRANSVERSAL}=\{\mathsf{I},\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{CNOT},\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{X}},\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{Z}}\}

(7.75)

(and their classically controlled analogs, if unitary) such that

•

The gadgets for
\mathsf{I},\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{CNOT}
and their classically controlled analogs has input and output code types
\mathsf{qLDPC}_{{1/5}}^{({i})}
. The gadgets for
\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{X}},\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{Z}}
has input code type
\mathsf{qLDPC}_{{1/5}}^{({i})}
and trivial output code type.

•

Each gadget has width
O(k_{i})
and (identical) depth
O(d_{i})
.

•

For each operation,
\mathsf{g}\in\mathsf{CSSTRANSVERSAL}
, the family of bad fault paths of the corresponding gadget
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{g}}
has weight enumerator bounded on
x\in[0,\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{qLDPCTRANSVERSAL}}]
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{g}};~x\right)}\leq e^{-\beta d_{i}}O(\mathsf{poly}(d_{i}))

(7.76)

for some constant
\beta>0
.

Proof.

All CSS codes have transversal implementations of the operations in
\mathsf{CSSTRANSVERSAL}
.
The construction and proof is identical to
Section

7.1
with parameters modified in the obvious way.
∎

{lemma}

[qLDPC resource state gadget]
Let
k_{i}
be the number of qubits encoded by

and
d_{i}
its distance.
Consider

registers of size

and let

be an

-qubit state prepared by a Clifford+T circuit

of width

and depth

.
42
42

42

Depth and
m

O(1)
here means an absolute constant such as
10
.

There exists an absolute constant
\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{qLDPC~PREP}}\in(0,1)

\mathsf{PREP}_{\psi}^{i}

(C_{\psi},\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=O(k_{i}~d_{i}^{5.91}~\mathrm{polylog}(d_{i}))

(7.77)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=O(d_{i}^{2}~\mathrm{polylog}(d_{i}))~.

(7.78)

On
x\in[0,\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{qLDPC~PREP}}]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F};~x\right)}\leq\mathsf{poly}(d_{i})e^{-d_{i}/10}~.

(7.79)

Construction.

\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{qLDPC}({i})}^{\mathsf{CLIFF}}

]

.
43
43

. The precise choice of logical basis for

Let
C^{\prime}

The gadget is the application of

to

with

to be selected later and
\mathcal{U}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathsf{qLDPC}_{{1/5}}^{({i})}
44
44

44

We attach the code type
\mathsf{qLDPC}_{{1/5}}^{({i})}
to bundles of qubits originating from each application of

\mathcal{U}
is the sum of
\mathsf{qLDPC}_{{1/5}}^{({i})}
imposed on each bundle of output qubits.
to obtain a
(C^{\prime},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{U}})
-FT gadget
\mathsf{PREP}_{\psi}^{i}
.

\blacktriangleleft

Proof.

Let
\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{PREP}}\in(0,1)
be the threshold value from the statement of

.
From

, for some absolute constant

can be bounded on

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F};~x\right)}\leq\epsilon_{\mathrm{prep}}+m\cdot\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathsf{qLDPC}_{{1/5}}^{({i})};~c\cdot x\right)}~.

(7.81)

Let
d_{i}
be the distance of
\mathsf{qLDPC}({i})
, we can apply

to bound the weight enumerator of the code type as

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathsf{qLDPC}_{{1/5}}^{({i})};~c\cdot x\right)}\leq\mathsf{poly}(d_{i})\left(O_{\Delta}(x)\right)^{d_{i}/10}~.

(7.82)

Let

be a small enough constant so that the term in the base of the exponent is at most

, so that on

We now set

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F};~x\right)}\leq O(1)~f(d_{i})~.

(7.84)

was invoked on a constant depth circuit of width

with

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=O(\log^{2}(V^{\prime})~\mathrm{polyloglog}(V^{\prime}))=O(d_{i}^{2}~\mathrm{polylog}(d_{i}))~.\qed

(7.86)

{corollary}

[qLDPC gate gadget]
There exists a constant
\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{GATE}}\in(0,1)
such that for a one or two-qubit operation
\mathsf{g}
in the set

\displaystyle\mathsf{GATESET}=\{\mathsf{T},\mathsf{I}(A),\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y},\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{CNOT},\mathsf{H},\mathsf{S},\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{X}}(A),\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{Z}}(A),\mathsf{INIT}_{\mathsf{Z}}(A)\}

(7.87)

with support on qubits from at most two registers of size
k_{i}
,
there exists a circuit
\mathsf{GATE}_{\mathsf{g}}
that is a
(\mathsf{g},\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{g}})
-FT gadget with input and output types
\mathsf{qLDPC}_{{1/5}}^{({i})}
.
On
x\in[0,\epsilon_{*,\mathsf{GATE}}]
the weight enumerator of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{g}}
is bounded as

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}{\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{g}};~x\right)}\leq\mathsf{poly}(d_{i})~e^{-\beta d_{i}}~.

(7.88)

\mathsf{GATE}_{\mathsf{g}}
has width
W
and depth
D
where

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=O(k_{i}~d_{i}^{5.91}~\mathrm{polylog}(d_{i}))

(7.89)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=O(d_{i}^{2}~\mathrm{polylog}(d_{i}))~.

(7.90)

\mathsf{GATE}_{\mathsf{I}(A)}
(identity) has width
W=O(k_{i})
.
The depth is independent of
\mathsf{g}
.

Construction.
For a gate
\mathsf{g}=\mathsf{GATESET}\setminus\{\mathsf{I}(A),\mathsf{INIT}_{\mathsf{Z}}(A),\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{X}}(A),\mathsf{M}_{\mathsf{Z}}(A)\}
supported on
m
registers, we use
Section

7.3
to prepare
\ket{\mathsf{g}}=(\mathsf{g}\otimes I)\ket{\phi_+}^{mk_{i}}
where
\mathsf{g}
should be interpreted as acting on one side of the Bell pair.
Let
t\leq 3
be the level of the Clifford hierarchy that
\mathsf{g}
is in.

\ket{\mathsf{g}}
can be consumed by a teleportation circuit using operations only in
\mathsf{CSSTRANSVERSAL}
and one operation in level
t^{\prime}=\max(t-1,1)
of the Clifford hierarchy
[
GC99
]
.
Let
C_{\mathsf{g}}
be the circuit that inductively performs the previous procedure until
